Title: UltraFlash: Accelerating Megapixel Visual Synthesis

  • Authors: Phuc Lai, Anh Nguyen, Phong Nguyen, Anh Tran
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33581
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.33581
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.33581]] UltraFlash: Accelerating Megapixel Visual Synthesis(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33581)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Megapixel visual synthesis with latent diffusion models requires operating far beyond the resolutions at which these models are typically trained. Cascade refinement is a leading strategy for this setting: it starts from a base image at a native resolution and progressively upsamples it to the target resolution through multiple refinement stages. Each stage typically performs a pixel-space resolution transition followed by latent-space denoising. While prior acceleration efforts mainly focus on reducing denoising cost, we identify a complementary and underexplored bottleneck: the repeated movement of representations through the VAE. We call these operations pixel-space transitions. In cascade pipelines, intermediate latents are often decoded to RGB, resized, and re-encoded before refinement, while final latents are decoded patch by patch with dense overlap to suppress boundary artifacts. We propose UltraFlash, a transition-efficient approach that accelerates these VAE-heavy operations. UltraFlash has two components. Latent Hyperloop replaces intermediate RGB round trips with a learned latent-space shortcut that approximates the decode-resize-encode transformation without repeated VAE decoding and encoding. Sparse Patch-based Decoding accelerates final reconstruction by addressing patch seams caused by inconsistent GroupNorm statistics and reusing cached statistics across patches, enabling sparse VAE decoding with much smaller overlap. Integrated into a state-of-the-art cascade baseline, UltraFlash reduces 4K generation latency by approximately 2x while improving image quality. These results show that optimizing pixel-space transitions can improve both efficiency and fidelity, offering an effective route toward fast, high-quality megapixel visual synthesis.

  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: Multilingual BabyLM systems usually fix language sampling proportions even though the weakest language can change during training. Controlled experiments show that equal character budgets create a severe representation artifact for Chinese; byte-level encoding removes much of it, while static Chinese protection can subsequently make English the weakest language. These results support a simple conclusion: multilingual weakness is a training-time state rather than a fixed property of a language. Conversely, standard deep classifiers that are amenable to federated optimization often suffer from catastrophic forgetting under label distribution skew. Furthermore, leveraging this insight, we propose a novel One-Shot "Product of Experts" protocol that enables privacy-preserving aggregation without iterative synchronization. Finally, we mechanistically analyze the trade-off between interpolation strategies, demonstrating that while linear mixup offers an enticing computational shortcut, it destabilizes optimization gradients by orders of magnitude ($10^9\times$). Across five tabular benchmarks and four levels of label skew, Geodesic-SVDD attains the highest mean AUROC among seven federated methods, outperforms federated tree ensembles by up to 21 AUROC points on Covertype, and its One-Shot variant matches centralized Deep SVDD under IID and mild skew with a single communication round.

Title: Beyond Affine Reconstruction: Multi-Resolution Transformer DeepONets for the Radiative Transfer Equation

In this paper, we develop a transformer-based DeepONet for multi-resolution RTE surrogate modelling. This architecture allows a single model to process inputs at varying resolutions without resampling them onto a common grid. We formulate a computable empirical version of a previously derived theoretical lower bound for affine reconstruction, enabling direct comparison with model errors on the same dataset. Power spectral density analysis shows improved preservation of small-scale structures. Experiments on a dataset combining three spatial resolutions confirm the accuracy advantage of the approach at each input resolution.

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.33688
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.33688]] Rescuing Your Pruning: Rethinking Visual Token Pruning and Post-Training Recovery in MLLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33688)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Visual token pruning substantially reduces the inference cost of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), yet existing methods are mostly training-free and plug-and-play. They treat the resulting performance drop as an inevitable cost of compression and pursue increasingly complex pruning rules. We rethink this view through two questions: how much of the loss can post-training rescue, and, building on this recovery, how can joint compression push pruning to its limit? We curate a 282K-sample post-training mixture covering general VQA, reasoning, OCR, and grounding, and post-train 36 configurations that drop or merge tokens under a unified protocol. Post-training rescues most of the loss, even for OCR and grounding, and its adaptation gain in closing the gap between the pruned and the dense model grows as the budget tightens. For joint compression, we find that the vision encoder, not the LLM, dominates prefill time in native-resolution MLLMs, so where tokens are removed matters more than how many: keeping 40% of tokens at the encoder runs faster than keeping 10% at the projector. Our best configuration prunes the encoder lightly and the projector aggressively, leaves the LLM intact, and keeps general understanding and reasoning at the dense level. It accelerates the LLM stage by 3.49x but the end-to-end time-to-first-token by only 1.54x, showing that LLM-stage speedups overstate end-to-end gains. We hope these findings offer insights for future research on joint visual token compression. Our data, protocol, and code will be released.

  • Abstract: On Riemannian manifolds, generative-model evaluation is geometry-dependent since curvature and dimension jointly determine distances and volume growth. Title: A Distribution-Matching Perspective on LLM Training Algorithms

  • Authors: Yanxing Huang, Bingzhang Yan, Zihan Tang, Wenzhe Li, Peng Li, Yang Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33740
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.33740
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.33740]] A Distribution-Matching Perspective on LLM Training Algorithms(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33740)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: In this work, we propose a unified framework for training algorithms across different stages of modern large language model development, based on the perspective of distribution matching. We show that a broad class of language model training algorithms can be interpreted as minimizing a certain divergence between the current model and a target distribution, and that characterizing the properties of this target distribution provides substantial insight into the behavior of the corresponding training algorithms. By analyzing specific forms of the target distribution, we establish that On-Policy Distillation (OPD) enjoys an advantage over classical Knowledge Distillation (KD) when the current model distribution is close to the target distribution, while exhibiting a disadvantage when the two distributions are far apart. Furthermore, when the target distribution itself depends on the model being optimized, we show that the accumulation of estimation variance can induce a deterministic degradation of the target distribution. These results provide a theoretical explanation, at least in part, for the long-term behavior of existing Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms. With this intuition we further designed Conditional KL (CKL) regularization for mitigating the degradation of the target distribution. CKL can effectively preserve mode diversity while relaxing the constraint imposed by classical KL regularization on reward improvements. We believe this theoretical perspective can bring some new understanding of language model training algorithms and provide useful guidance for future research.

Title: Ring-0 by Default: Agents as Operating Systems (AaOS)

  • Authors: Xiang Li, Aichun Huang, Zuyao Xu, Fasheng Miao, Zheli Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.OS
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33753
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.33753
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.33753]] Ring-0 by Default: Agents as Operating Systems (AaOS)(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33753)
  • Keywords: protect, defense
  • Abstract: LLM agents now read and write files, start processes and subagents, fetch network content, load third-party extensions, and save and resume state, which are the responsibilities of an operating system. They gained these functions within about three years, but not the protections that operating systems built over five decades: whatever the model outputs, whether it follows the user, a tool result, or a downloaded skill, runs with the full authority of the host user, in effect at ring 0. Prior work builds an operating system for agents (OS4A); we argue that a deployed agent already is like an operating system (AaOS) and analyze it as one. Taking the mechanisms of standard OS textbooks as a checklist, we map 116 mechanisms in 19 subsystems to their agent counterparts, from product documentation, source code, and the literature, and label each as isomorphic, degraded (function present, protection dependent on the model), missing, mutated, or novel. Only 11 are isomorphic and 65 are missing, most of them protections: privilege separation, resource quotas, transactions, and tamper-proof audit. Existing defenses confine the whole agent or review its extensions and leave this interior unprotected, and by Anderson's reference-monitor criteria (non-bypassable, tamper-proof, verifiable), rules written in the prompt satisfy none. We test the map with thirteen experiments on Claude Code, Codex CLI, and Gemini CLI, covering 25 mechanisms whose enforcement documentation cannot settle: 16 match the analysis, 7 gain a more precise description, and 2 change verdict. Checks sit in single tools, so the shell bypasses them; all three CLIs load forged transcript records on resume; and task control, sub-agent permissions, and model-based monitors are weak. We identify five problems for which no OS defense transfers and derive a prioritized list of open problems from the gaps of the map.

Title: TriageRAG: Failure-Aware Self-Training for Adapting Retrieval-Augmented Language Models to Specialized Domains

  • Authors: Samarth Varshney, Ritu Yadav
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.IR, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33776
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.33776
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.33776]] TriageRAG: Failure-Aware Self-Training for Adapting Retrieval-Augmented Language Models to Specialized Domains(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33776)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) lets large language models (LLMs) answer knowledge-intensive questions from retrieved evidence, but adapting a general-purpose RAG system to a specialized domain such as medicine is difficult when only an unlabeled corpus is available. Self-training approaches such as SimRAG address this by letting the LLM write its own question-answer pairs from domain passages and fine-tuning on the pairs that survive a round-trip consistency filter. We observe that these approaches treat every retained example identically: they never ask whether the student already knows the answer, already extracts it correctly from the retrieved context, or fails - and if so, whether the failure lies with the reader or with the retriever. To tackle this, we propose TriageRAG, a failure-aware self-training approach that probes the student on each self-generated question with and without retrieved context, triages it into one of four categories (known, solved, context-failure, retrieval-failure), and routes each category to the training signal that addresses it, including a parametric route that teaches the reader to answer when retrieval misses. Probing is cheap and repeated every round, so the training mixture follows the student, and the triage histogram doubles as a label-free diagnostic of where the domain gap lies. Experiments on 11 datasets spanning three domains with a Qwen3-4B backbone, and on CS-Bench with Qwen3-8B, demonstrate that TriageRAG outperforms a budget-matched SimRAG by 1.2-6.4 points and is the only method that recovers the closed-book level in every case where retrieval hurts the base model. A single ablation identifies the mechanism: round-trip filtering removes every training context that lacks the evidence, and a reader that never sees such contexts learns to over-trust noisy retrieval.

Title: Segmentation of Moving Objects During Camera Motion Using the OWL Function

  • Authors: Daniel Raviv, Juan D. Yepes
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33789
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.33789
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.33789]] Segmentation of Moving Objects During Camera Motion Using the OWL Function(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33789)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: We present a principled, analytical framework for segmenting multiple independently moving rigid objects during single camera motion, built directly upon the OWL perception function. OWL encodes two fundamental visual motion cues, perceived looming $L$ and perceived rotation $\omega$, into a single closed-form representation of relative 3D structure. A key consequence of this structure is that the unit direction of relative translation $\hat{t}$ is analytically recoverable per image point from $L$ and $\omega$ alone. All points belonging to a non-rotating rigid body share the same $\hat{t}$, whereas points on different rigid bodies generally yield distinct values. This furnishes a natural segmentation criterion: clustering per-point $\hat{t}$ estimates partitions the visible scene into independently moving rigid bodies. Building on the 2D complex form of OWL, we derive the framework in 3D using quaternions, for a single moving camera. The approach is parallel, camera rotation-invariant, and requires no training data, no depth information, and no semantic priors.

Title: MTA-Zero: Self-Improving Multimodal Deep Search via Online QA-Solver Co-training

  • Authors: Xiangyu Peng, Jiaxin Zhang, An Yan, Yu Li, Ran Xu, Zeyuan Chen, Chien-Sheng Wu
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33799
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.33799
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.33799]] MTA-Zero: Self-Improving Multimodal Deep Search via Online QA-Solver Co-training(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33799)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have become strong visual reasoners, yet remain limited in open-world, multi-step search that requires grounding images, selecting tools, and integrating web evidence. A separate deep-search Solver then selects tools, gathers evidence, and answers the generated questions. We optimize the two policies independently with GRPO, without sharing parameters or gradients. Across six multimodal deep-search benchmarks, MTA-Zero improves a Qwen3-VL-8B Solver from 36.91% to 51.58% mean accuracy and achieves performance comparable to GPT-5 under the same agent workflow. The same framework also improves a newer Qwen3.6-27B Solver by 10.82 points. These results show that verified online question generation provides a policy-specific curriculum for continually improving multimodal deep-search agents.

Comparisons of low-rank adaptation (LoRA) with full fine-tuning are common but often confounded, and task format is rarely studied. We compare both regimes on prompt-safety classification with a matched 400M encoder-decoder pair (Ettin) across three task formats, with up to 15 seeds per cell and pre-specified analyses. At the pre-specified learning rates, full fine-tuning leads LoRA by 2-3 pp in every architecture and format trained on the label alone. On the encoder, the gap closes once both rates are chosen by the same search. Training LoRA to write a reason after its verdict adds 4.0 pp and closes the gap, but a content-free sentence of equal length does not. Trained models score above a prompted 8B in domain but fall behind it on two of three out-of-distribution corpora, trailing by 8-23 pp on adversarial jailbreaks and flagging 27-66% of benign but harmful-looking prompts as unsafe, against the 8B's 2-3%. Together, the results suggest that the adaptation regime matters less than how each model is trained, its learning rate, and training target, and that in-domain accuracy alone is an incomplete guide to robustness.

  • Abstract: A central challenge in multivariate forecasting is deciding what to share across channels and where to specialize. Through controlled comparisons across eleven datasets and three backbones, we show that informative channel assignments improve forecasting beyond matched shared capacity and random allocation. Title: Ranking Is Not Deciding: Benchmark Shortcuts and Operating-Point Collapse in Query-Time RAG Poisoning Defenses

  • Authors: Pushkal Kumar, Arvin Sahaym
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33849
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.33849
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.33849]] Ranking Is Not Deciding: Benchmark Shortcuts and Operating-Point Collapse in Query-Time RAG Poisoning Defenses(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33849)
  • Keywords: defense, attack
  • Abstract: Query-time filters that screen each retrieved document before it reaches the generator are the dominant published defense against retrieval-augmented generation poisoning, and they report near-perfect detection on domain benchmarks. We show that this performance is predicted by how the poison set was built rather than by what the filter reads: removing a single construction cue destroys the deployed decision before it destroys the ranking. Two label-free statistics of a poison set and its clean corpus profile the cue: containment, how often the target question appears verbatim in the poison, and length overlap. The ranking survives; the decision does not. A defense-aware attacker defeats our own scorer, so we claim no defense. A detection result is evidence only when it is reported with its poison set's construction profile and the false-positive rate at the threshold actually deployed; we release the script that computes the profile.

Title: Securing Edge & Physical AI: Post-Quantum IoT Blockchain for Mission-Critical Cyber-Physical Agents

  • Abstract: Distributed Cyber-Physical Systems (CPS) and Edge AI networks increasingly depend on public-key authentication and key-establishment mechanisms that are vulnerable to cryptographically relevant Quantum Computers (QC). As a result, resource-constrained agents can take part as lightweight clients, and low-latency operational traffic stays outside the ledger consensus path, protected by the currently active symmetric epoch keys. Our results show that ledger-assisted post-quantum key-epoch management is feasible for selected Edge AI and CPS security-control workloads under the stated trust, compromise, connectivity, and resource assumptions.

Title: Stable Dimensionality Reduction via Isometric Centroid-Encoder

  • Authors: Tomojit Ghosh, Siva Rama Krishna Prasad Changala, Prathyusha Kanakamalla, Harigovind Harikumar
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33872
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.33872
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.33872]] Stable Dimensionality Reduction via Isometric Centroid-Encoder(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33872)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Many supervised dimensionality reduction methods promote intra-class compactness and inter-class separation but do not explicitly constrain class geometry, leading to variability in the relative arrangement of classes across independent runs. Such variability limits the reproducibility and interpretability of low-dimensional embeddings. We propose the Isometric Centroid-Encoder (ICE), a supervised dimensionality reduction framework in which class centroids are learned directly in the latent space while preserving a prescribed pairwise class-centroid geometry. Unlike existing centroid- or prototype-based methods that primarily encourage intra-class compactness, ICE explicitly regularizes the relative arrangement of latent class centroids. Unlike multidimensional scaling and many metric-learning approaches that preserve sample-level relationships, ICE operates on class centroids, preserving inter-class geometry without requiring large numbers of sample-pair or triplet constraints. We further show that the intrinsic dimensionality required to represent the centroid-distance configuration of $M$ classes is at most $M-1$, explaining the observed saturation of distance-preservation measures as the latent dimensionality increases. To quantify embedding reproducibility, we introduce the Centroid Neighborhood Stability (CNS) metric, which measures the consistency of class-level neighborhood topology across independent runs and complements existing embedding quality measures. Experiments on image and biological datasets demonstrate that ICE produces stable embeddings with improved preservation of class-centroid geometry while remaining computationally efficient. Additional analyses of optimization dynamics, sensitivity, and preprocessing further provide practical insights into latent dimensionality and explicit geometry modeling for supervised dimensionality reduction.

Title: Semantic Concept Steering Breaks the Explanation Drift Loop in Continual Learning

  • Authors: Yehonatan Elisha, Oren Barkan, Noam Koenigstein
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33877
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.33877
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.33877]] Semantic Concept Steering Breaks the Explanation Drift Loop in Continual Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33877)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Catastrophic forgetting in continual learning (CL) manifests not only as accuracy degradation but also as \emph{explanation drift}: models maintain predictive accuracy while silently shifting attention from diagnostic features to spurious correlations. This work identifies and resolves a previously overlooked structural flaw in explanation-aware CL: pixel-level saliency regularization derives supervision targets from the model's own frozen saliency maps, creating a \emph{self-referential drift loop} that actively reinforces spurious attention across tasks. We introduce \textbf{C$^3$L} (Concept-Consistent Continual Learning), which breaks this loop by anchoring regularization to class-consistent semantic concepts (e.g., ``curved beak'', ``striped pattern'') discovered offline, independent of model state, providing (i) target independence from model drift, and (ii) cross-instance semantic consistency across every instance of a class. We identify that our concept-based objective, combined with approximate LRP relevance conservation, induces a \emph{crowding-out effect}: since concept and background regions compete for the relevance of the same prediction, enforcing high relevance within concept regions implicitly suppresses spurious background correlations without explicit penalization. \method does not require human-annotated concept sets, as it employs a fully automated concept discovery and spatial grounding. Extensive evaluation across five benchmarks and over 20 baselines demonstrates state-of-the-art performance in accuracy, forgetting, explanation quality, spurious correlation mitigation, and out-of-distribution robustness.

Title: Counterfactual Test-Time Augmentation for Shortcut Removal in Language Models

  • Authors: Katie Matton, Rosalind W. Picard, John Guttag, Maggie Makar
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33904
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.33904
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.33904]] Counterfactual Test-Time Augmentation for Shortcut Removal in Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33904)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can exploit shortcuts, i.e., correlations present in training data that may not hold at test time. This can lead to poor predictions when correlations shift. Most existing solutions for removing shortcuts operate at training time, requiring labeled training data, model weight access, and significant computational resources. Here, we study the problem of test-time shortcut removal. We propose a family of counterfactual test-time augmentation (CTTA) methods: given an example, each method uses an auxiliary model to generate counterfactual edits in which the shortcut value is changed, and then takes a weighted average of the LLM's predictions across these edits. We prove that the optimal shortcut-invariant predictor is a member of this CTTA family, and we characterize its weights. The weights depend on the training distribution, and therefore are, in general, not identifiable from the trained model and unlabeled test data. However, we present assumptions under which the weights can be obtained using the token probabilities of the trained LLM, and propose tests to detect violations of these assumptions. We use these tests to develop Select-CTTA, an aggregate method that selects among CTTA methods. Empirically, we find that Select-CTTA outperforms test-time baselines and approaches the performance of models fine-tuned on shortcut-balanced data.

Title: Hierarchical Cooperative GFlowNets

  • Authors: Pedro Dall'Antonia, Juan Belieni, Ana Carolina Erthal, Saullo H. G. de Oliveira, Eliezer de Souza da Silva, Diego Mesquita

  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) are trained to sample objects from compositional spaces with probability proportional to a reward function, but ineffective exploration can cause learned samplers to concentrate on a few high-reward regions. An intuitive remedy is to train multiple GFlowNets independently in parallel and combine their samples. However, this does not prevent multiple models from collapsing onto the same high-reward areas, leaving large regions of the state space unexplored. With this in mind, we propose Hierarchical Cooperative GFlowNets (HCG), an approach that dynamically partitions the search space to coordinate discovery without relying on hand-crafted boundaries. HCG imposes an ordering in which the highest-ranked model may explore the entire space, while lower-ranked models are progressively pushed toward regions not covered by higher-ranked ones. We provide a theoretical characterization of how specific learning objectives can shift probability mass away from designated areas, instantiating this mechanism within our architecture. A shared classifier coordinates the hierarchy by partitioning the search space, keeping the GFlowNets independently parameterized and enabling asynchronous training. Furthermore, we derive a normalizer-weighted aggregation rule for the ensemble and prove that it recovers the target reward-proportional distribution under the stated assumptions. Finally, experiments across multimodal distributions, sequence generation, combinatorial optimization, and biological sequence design show that HCG improves mode coverage and target approximation, translating increases in the number of coordinated samplers into broader, complementary exploration.

Title: Resolving the Mobility-Equity-Emissions Trilemma: A Causal Machine Learning Framework for Dynamic Tolling Policy

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Professionals send working documents to external large language models. In UK English it also restores at least 99% of its placeholders after 760 answers. In Russian it restores only 94.6% [93.6; 95.6] of name placeholders, against a 99% threshold, mainly because the inflection it borrows from pii-guard produces wrong surname forms. The engine was developed over six earlier held-out sets from the same generator; real documents were not tested.

  • Keywords: explainability
  • Abstract: As Vision-Language Models (VLMs) increasingly enable robots to follow language instructions, understanding their navigation decisions becomes important for reliable deployment. We identify instruction-stage insensitivity, where existing methods produce similar explanations despite changes across different navigation stages.   • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Transformers achieve strong performance across modalities but are bottlenecked by the quadratic cost of attention. Sparse attention can reduce this cost, but effective sparsity must preserve useful long-range communication and map well to GPU hardware. We introduce INTRA--Interleaved Non-contiguous Token spaRse Attention--a hardware-aware static sparse attention design based on a relay-communication view under Computational Query Set (CQS) constraints. Title: Identically Trained Language Models Break on Different Jailbreaks

  • Authors: Chi-Wei Chang, Mu-Tien Kuo, Kuan-Wei Lee, Meng-Hsun Tsai
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34074
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34074
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34074]] Identically Trained Language Models Break on Different Jailbreaks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34074)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Jailbreak benchmarks test one checkpoint and treat which jailbreaks break it as a property of its pretraining data and safety training. We ask whether another pretraining run of the same data, given the same safety training, would break on the same jailbreaks. For each of 25 pretraining data recipes, we take 1B base models from 3 pretraining seeds and give each the same safety post-training with 2 post-training seeds, for 150 models in all. We then collect 20 responses from every model to a shared set of jailbreak prompts and correlate the per-prompt non-refusal rates between every pair of models. On average, two post-trained copies of the same pretraining run have per-prompt non-refusal rates that correlate at 0.984. A different pretraining seed lowers the correlation to 0.811, and a different data recipe lowers it only slightly further, to 0.779. These results indicate that which jailbreaks break a checkpoint depends much more on its pretraining run than on its data, a pattern that holds in all 25 data recipes and at 150M and 300M parameters. In practice, the best of 91 attack templates found on one checkpoint stays the best after a new pretraining seed in only 27% of model pairs, against 67% after a new post-training seed. Each pretraining run also leaves a fingerprint: across 3,211 prompts, a model's per-prompt non-refusal rates identify its pretraining run among all 75 runs every time, and a random subset of 50 prompts still does so 98% of the time.

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Speculative decoding significantly accelerates autoregressive text generation in Large Language Models (LLMs), but its efficacy depends largely on two key factors: the distributional alignment between the draft and the target model, and the draft model's latency during inference. To address this gap, we formulate draft model design as an optimization problem and propose T2D, a framework that constructs draft models directly from the target model using latency-aware structured pruning and knowledge distillation. Title: Self-Evolution Reasoner: Continuous Optimization via Policy-Intrinsic Exploration and Exploitation

  • Authors: Wenhang Shi, Yiren Chen, Zhe Zhao, Jinhao Dong, Wei Lu, Xiaoyong Du

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34137

  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) enables large language models (LLMs) to continuously self-improve their reasoning capabilities. However, both paradigms suffer from exploration bottlenecks, struggling to escape local optima when the policy fails to sample valid solutions. Relying on external demonstrations can bypass this issue; however, the off-policy nature hinders genuine self-improvement and the mismatched-distribution reasoning can not be fully exploited. Specifically, SEPO utilizes labels to elicit correct reasoning from the policy, unlocking the exploration potential. Through this iterative cycle of policy-intrinsic exploration and exploitation, SEPO achieves robust and continuous capability evolution. Across scientific, tool-use, and complex logical reasoning domains, SEPO consistently achieves the best performance under identical training time or steps, demonstrating superior efficiency and convergence ceilings. Notably, SEPO maintains stable improvements even in scenarios where baselines severely underperform or fail entirely, such as on complex tasks or with small-scale models. Our code is available at this https URL.

Title: Reversion, Retention, and Semantic Intervention: Measuring Functional Content in CLIP Fine-Tuning Deltas

  • Authors: Chih-Ting Liao, Kezhen Bao, Xin Cao
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34144
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34144

  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Fine-tuning adapts pretrained models to new tasks, but identifying which weight changes support the resulting behavior remains difficult. Such descriptions leave open how much task performance depends on the identified updates, particularly when other updates can support the same predictions. We address this gap by pairing reversion, which restores selected parameters to their pretrained values, with retention, which keeps only selected fine-tuning updates. We evaluate Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining (CLIP) models on synthetic financial charts, Flowers102, EuroSAT, and FGVCAircraft with ViT-L/14, and on Flowers102 with ViT-B/16, using a shared recipe that tunes the final six visual blocks. Second, VO weights recover more loss improvement than all ten equally sized, layer-matched random subsets in each setting. VO and multilayer perceptron (MLP) retentions jointly correct 25-81% of pretrained errors, demonstrating overlapping recovered behavior, which we call operational redundancy. Third, removing semantically selected directions reduces VO-retained recovery more than matched-random removal in all five settings. Together, these results distinguish semantic alignment, removal cost, and retained recovery as complementary evidence about the functional role of fine-tuning updates within the evaluated CLIP recipe.

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) of LLMs suffers from catastrophic forgetting, and prevailing mitigation strategies struggle to reconcile the plasticity-stability dilemma. To address this, we propose Entropic Control for Homeostatic Optimization (ECHO), a closed-loop control framework. By decomposing the KL divergence, we identify output entropy as a dominant observable component of KL variation. This enables us to leverage entropy as a computationally efficient online proxy to monitor forgetting risks in real-time, bypassing expensive reference-model inference. Title: Timing Matters Twice: When to Query and When to Skip in Flow-Matching On-Policy Distillation

  • Authors: Renye Yan, Junyi Zhan, Jikang Cheng, Wei Peng
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34191
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34191
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34191]] Timing Matters Twice: When to Query and When to Skip in Flow-Matching On-Policy Distillation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34191)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) enables flow-matching models to acquire complementary capabilities from specialized teachers through supervision on student-generated trajectories. However, different evaluation dimensions, such as aesthetics and OCR accuracy, and different tasks, such as image generation and editing, emphasize different aspects of the denoising process, suggesting that their effective supervision may occur at different stages. Existing methods typically select supervision states from predefined timestep distributions, which may overlook these phase-dependent differences and incur unnecessary computational overhead. This motivates two critical timing decisions: when to adaptively query teachers for effective supervision based on the evolving generative process, and when to skip the remaining rollout to avoid redundant computation. In this paper, we introduce PhaseOPD, a phase-aware query-and-skip framework that identifies generative phases online from revisions of predicted-clean latents, using existing student velocity predictions without additional model evaluations. For each task-assigned teacher, PhaseOPD selects a supervision state based on its phase preference and skips the unused rollout suffix once all query decisions are finalized, preserving the single-query velocity-distillation objective. On SD3.5-M, PhaseOPD executes only 5.56 of 16 rollout positions per local batch, reducing training time by 41.3% relative to DanceOPD while improving Aesthetic, GenEval, and OCR scores.

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Knowledge distillation transfers reasoning capabilities from large language models to lightweight students. However, distilled small models still struggle with multi-step reasoning. Title: CORMA: Constraint-Routed Decision Mamba for Generalizable Multi-Constraint Offline Safe Reinforcement Learning

  • Authors: Changxu Ni, Feiran You, Yongan Guo, Hongyang Du, Kang Wei, Le Liang, Yiyang Ni, Jun Li
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34208
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34208
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34208]] CORMA: Constraint-Routed Decision Mamba for Generalizable Multi-Constraint Offline Safe Reinforcement Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34208)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Offline safe reinforcement learning (RL) learns safety-aware policies from static datasets while satisfying safety constraints during deployment. Motivated by the strong trajectorymodeling capabilities of Transformers, recent Decision Transformer (DT)-based methods formulate offline safe RL as conditional sequence modeling by conditioning action generation on return-to-go (RTG) and cost-to-go (CTG) tokens, thereby enabling adaptation to varying safety budgets. However, existing DT-based methods are developed for single-constraint tasks. For tasks with multiple constraints, generalizing learned policies to unseen combinations of safety budgets remains challenging due to the conflicting requirements imposed by heterogeneous constraints. Moreover, the quadratic complexity of Transformer architectures significantly increases the computational overhead in multi-constraint tasks, as representing additional constraints requires more CTG tokens. Recently, Mamba has emerged as an efficient sequence-modeling backbone, offering linear computational complexity and strong generalization capabilities. In this paper, we propose CORMA, a constraint-routed decision Mamba framework for generalizable multi-constraint offline safe RL. CORMA decomposes policy inference into constraint-aware RTG estimation and adaptive action generation, where constraint-specific experts model feasible RTG distributions and a router-guided product-of-experts mechanism fuses these distributions to provide safety-aware guidance for a Mamba-based action decoder. A three-stage optimization strategy is designed to improve training stability. Extensive experiments on OSRL benchmarks demonstrate that CORMA achieves superior reward-safety trade-offs, reduces inference latency by 37% compared with DT-based methods, and exhibits stronger zero-shot generalization to unseen safety-budget combinations than offline safe RL baselines.

Title: CropReward: A Human-Preference Benchmark and Reward Model for Aesthetic Image Cropping

  • Abstract: Aesthetic image cropping (AIC) aims to improve image composition and aesthetic quality by removing extraneous or distracting content. In addition, we employ CropReward to construct CropReward-300k, the largest AIC dataset to date, and further develop CropAlign, a preference-aligned AIC model trained solely with CropReward supervision, without human-annotated crop boxes. Experimental results demonstrate that CropReward aligns with human preferences significantly better than existing criteria. Title: HNSecW: A Logarithmic-Time MPC Protocol for Approximate Nearest Neighbor Search

  • Authors: Fahong Zhang, Weijie Jin, Lixu Wang, Ning Luo, Xiao Wang, Wei Dong
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.DB
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34248
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34248
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34248]] HNSecW: A Logarithmic-Time MPC Protocol for Approximate Nearest Neighbor Search(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34248)
  • Keywords: secure, privacy
  • Abstract: As a core component of modern RAG systems, approximate nearest neighbor (ANN) search has been extensively studied for decades. In practice, deploying ANN often requires symmetric privacy, where the querier's query and the database owner's data must remain hidden from each other, necessitating a secure multi-party computation (MPC) solution. So far, most MPC-based ANN protocols follow a cluster-based approach: they evaluate distances for the query over a large candidate set and rely on heavyweight primitives such as distributed ORAM to hide access patterns. As a result, each search typically requires $O(\sqrt{N})$ time. We propose HNSecW, a novel MPC protocol for ANN search that achieves the $O(\log N)$ search time for the first time. We first study the single-query setting. Starting from HNSW, the state-of-the-art ANN algorithm, we redesign its control flow and data structures to achieve fully data-independent execution. To further achieve logarithmic search time, we employ offline reshuffling and partial database duplication. Furthermore, we extend our protocol to support multiple sequential queries by introducing a "double visited" caching structure that eliminates redundant cross-query memory accesses, thereby avoiding per-query online shuffling. In addition, we develop a batch-query protocol that exploits higher parallelism to reduce both round complexity and the circuit's critical path. Our experiments show that HNSecW preserves the high accuracy of plaintext HNSW ($\geq 90\%$ recall) and delivers over a $20\times$ efficiency improvement over prior work on million-scale datasets.

Title: NameTrap: Steering AI Coding Agents Toward Security Vulnerabilities via Variable Names

  • Authors: Chieh-Yuan Chang, Guan-Wei Chen, Chia-Yi Hsu, Chih Yang, Chia-Mu Yu, Chun-Ying Huang
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34253

  • Keywords: security, defense, attack
  • Abstract: AI coding agents routinely clone repositories, install packages, and read source code obtained from the Web, so publicly hosted code becomes part of the context that shapes the code they write. We present NameTrap, a source-code poisoning attack that only renames identifiers in third-party Python files to steer AI coding agents toward attacker-targeted Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) vulnerabilities. An attacker-side coding agent iteratively aligns identifiers with a CWE-specific naming convention and confirms through local probe completions that the renamed file elicits the target weakness, while the released bait contains neither instructions nor vulnerable statements. Moreover, three AI-based security analyzers and four static analyzers report none of the targeted weaknesses in the poisoned files. These findings expose an overlooked attack surface of agents that consume Web content: seemingly harmless variable names in untrusted, Web-hosted code can compromise the security of downstream software.

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34305
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34305
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34305]] Learning Discriminative Prototypes via Boundary Repulsion MixUp for Object Re-Identification(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34305)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Object re-identification requires embeddings that are robust to intra-class variation in viewpoint, illumination, and occlusion, yet preserve sharp separation between visually similar identities. The hardest errors arise precisely where confusable identities meet, a region that pairwise losses shape only implicitly. We present PBR-ReID, a prototype-based framework that makes this inter-class geometry an explicit learning target. Each identity is represented by a learnable prototype jointly optimized with a DiNOv3 backbone and the resulting prototype similarity matrix reveals, at every training step, which identities are most confusable. On this signal we build Boundary Repulsion MixUp (BRM), a geometry-aware regularizer that converts standard MixUp's weakness into a discriminative signal. Rather than interpolating samples across confusable identities, which blurs the boundaries that matter most, BRM constructs an explicit boundary proxy between each confusable pair and repels their features away from it, converting boundary ambiguity into a discriminative signal. We adopt a two-stage training protocol consisting of a prototype warmup phase followed by EMA-stabilized joint fine-tuning. Extensive experiments on person and vehicle ReID benchmarks demonstrate strong performance, with thorough ablations confirming each component's contribution. Code is available at this https URL.

Title: Birth, Death and Diff: Insertion-Based Sequence Generation with Corrections

  • Authors: Durga Prasad Maram, Dhruvesh Patel, Tahira Naseem, Benjamin Rozonoyer, Gaurav Pandey, Ramón Fernandez Astudillo, Andrew McCallum
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34310
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34310
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34310]] Birth, Death and Diff: Insertion-Based Sequence Generation with Corrections(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34310)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Insertion-based masked diffusion models enable flexible-length, parallel generation by interleaving mask insertion with token unmasking. However, because their generation decisions are irreversible, aggressive parallel decoding can introduce errors that subsequent steps cannot correct, necessitating conservative decoding with more forward passes (NFEs). Moreover, they exhibit higher entropy than standard MDMs because they marginalize over multiple alignments during training and therefore require even more conservative decoding in the absence of a correction mechanism. To address this limitation, we introduce ${\delta}$MDM, which makes intermediate generations revisable by equipping an insertion-based model with substitution and deletion operations. During training, we expose the model to errors caused by aggressive unmasking and insertion, teaching it to correct its own mistakes. At inference time, we interleave insertion and unmasking with correction steps that can revise previously unmasked tokens and remove unnecessary masks. This mechanism makes aggressive decoding practical, improving both generation speed and quality. ${\delta}$MDM Pareto-dominates FlexMDM, a vanilla insertion-based MDM, on the speed--quality trade-off. On high-diversity unconditional text generation on OpenWebText, it halves the generative perplexity relative to FlexMDM at the highest inference budget. It also achieves lower generative perplexity at all inference budgets compared to MDM trained from scratch with variable-length padded sequences. On low-diversity conditional text generation on TinyGSM, it matches FlexMDM accuracy with half as many NFEs.

Title: TopX: Text-to-Motion for Unseen Skeletons from the Skeleton File Alone

  • Authors: Tengjiao Sun, Pengcheng Fang, Xiaoyu Zhan, Dongjie Fu, Xiaohao Cai, Hansung Kim
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34321
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34321
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34321]] TopX: Text-to-Motion for Unseen Skeletons from the Skeleton File Alone(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34321)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Text-to-motion models generate for the skeletons they were trained on, while a new character arrives as a skeleton file and nothing else. We study text-to-motion for skeletons that no training clip has used, with no clips of the character, no template, no retargeting and no fine-tuning. We present TopX, a 73M-parameter flow-matching transformer that generates joint rotations and a root trajectory for any kinematic tree from its tree, rest pose and joint names. Motion is normalised by the rest pose and the size of the skeleton, and the rest pose enters as a world-frame descriptor that does not depend on how the rig's author oriented each joint, with rest conventions randomised in training. Attention is factorised over the joints and the frames of a motion and carries a spatial--temporal encoding designed for skeleton graphs of any topology: queries and keys are rotated across joints by a heat-kernel coordinate of the skeleton graph, which is defined for every tree and does not change with the order of its joints or the eigenbasis of its Laplacian, and across frames by a rotary encoding of frame order. To measure what this buys, we build SkelShift: four motion corpora under one codec, 143,334 captioned clips on skeletons of 4 to 142 joints, from which 4,232 clips on 2,043 skeletons are held out by clip, rig, tree class or species and organised along topology, joint count, body plan, species and rig source, and scored by motion quality: Fréchet distances on public kinetic features and on kinematic descriptors, foot skating and ground contact. Against UniMate trained on the same clips, on test rigs without a training clip, TopX lowers the descriptor FID from 5.73 to 4.58 when UniMate's guidance is tuned for the kinetic FID, and the kinetic FID from 1.48 to 0.55 when it is tuned for the descriptor FID, also on motions no training skeleton performs; fewer of its frames sink below the ground.

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can act as decision models that return a probability distribution over a fixed set of options, but deployed decision models face domain, label-prior, emerging-class and candidate-set shift. Title: CoLink: Global Compression and Knowledge Linking for Multi-Turn Dialogue Modeling

  • Authors: Lian Yan, Zhenbo Xia, Haotian Wang, Chen Tang, Yi Guan, Jie Liu, Jingchi Jiang
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Extracting salient information from long-horizon dialogue histories remains a significant challenge for Large Language Models (LLMs) due to the restricted context windows and efficient retrieval challenges. Current methods typically rely on history organization or local token-level optimization, suffering from information loss, retrieval redundancy, and myopic attention. Inspired by the view in cognitive science that dialogue constitutes sense-making and memory association, we propose CoLink, a framework that enhances LLMs' global context awareness in domain-specific multi-turn dialogues through information linking and compression. Coupled with a competitive slot mechanism, CoLink condenses the global context into dedicated memory and knowledge slots, efficiently distilling lengthy, redundant inputs into an extremely compact latent representation. Extensive empirical results on both public and our MMD datasets validate CoLink's superiority and generalizability in knowledge linking and sense modeling.

Title: ScaleRLT: Exact Log-Probabilities and Fast Deferred Rollouts for Reinforcement Learning on Looped Transformers

  • Authors: Dezhen Lu, Zixiang Ni, Runguo Li
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.DC

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34427

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looped language models such as Ouro reuse one block of layers several times per token. A rollout engine can batch the deeper loops of several response positions and run them only when a deep readout is due. This deferred schedule is fast, but the learner of an on-policy RL algorithm recomputes the sampled tokens' log-probabilities with a dense pass, and the two computations differ in floating point: in FP32 at 4096 tokens about 18% of the sampled tokens miss a $10^{-5}$ same-weight gate, and with bf16 weights nearly every token does. We use a hybrid learner log-probability, $\ell=\ell_{\mathrm{dense}}+\mathrm{sg}(\ell_{\mathrm{sched}}-\ell_{\mathrm{dense}})$, where $\mathrm{sg}$ denotes stop-gradient. Its value is a no-grad replay through the rollout's own schedule, and its gradient is the dense learner's. The replay reproduces the rollout bit for bit in all 167 FP32 and 28 bf16 real-weight cells we ran on three platforms, including rollouts whose batch shrinks at stop tokens, and the gradient has the same FP32 error as other dense implementations in all 102 pre-registered comparisons from 96 to 4096 tokens; the full GRPO loss of a training step matches a float64 reference to $3.1\times10^{-4}$. On one GPU, deferral raises rollout throughput by $1.47$ to $1.56\times$, and by $1.18$ to $1.54\times$ once every step runs from CUDA graphs, bit for bit. In a two-node GRPO loop, one-wave KV admission and CUDA graphs shorten an update by $2.71\times$ with the gate bitwise at every update, and four nodes keep the update time within 2% at the same per-GPU load. In 100-update runs held-out GSM8K accuracy rose from 23% to 82% with the mixed-depth readout that deferral relies on and from 34% to 90% with the native readout, which reached a higher accuracy in less time. For looped models beyond one node we analyse a ring mapping of layers to GPUs and measure its communication paths.

Title: CyberDual: AI Attackers versus AI Code Reviewers in Real Software Maintenance

  • Authors: Chenyuan Yang, Hwiwon Lee, Jongseong Kim, Yihan Yang, Dongjun Kim, Yinfang Chen, Lingming Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.SE

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Open-source projects now let AI agents review incoming code, but the code under review may itself come from an AI attacker. We present CyberDual, an arena for this dual role of AI: attacker agents hide an exploitable bug in real maintenance work on widely used open-source projects, and reviewer agents try to find and repair it. Execution decides each match: we rerun the attacker's proof of concept (PoC) on the code that comes out of review. Every attack is new, and the windows can be rebuilt each quarter from the latest upstream history. Three agentic reviewers catch 85-91% of 1,088 valid attacks from six attackers. Beyond a leaderboard, CyberDual has three uses. First, PoC execution gives both sides a reward that agent reports cannot game, so its surviving attacks can train reviewers against stronger attackers. Second, it can gate a release on both scores: Claude Sonnet 5 refuses one in five attack slots, yet attacks about as well as GPT-6-Sol, so refusal alone does not remove attack capability. Third, it can warn the teams behind a deployed reviewer when a new attacker crosses a threshold: GLM 5.3 in Claude Code already gets an exploit past Sonnet 5 in 17% of its attack slots.

Title: Task Relevance-Driven Prompt Expansion for Federated Continual Learning

  • Authors: Jiahua Dong, Wenqi Liang, Yang Cong, Yichen Li, Hongyuan Yu, Henghui Ding, Yulun Zhang, Salman Khan, Fahad Shahbaz Khan
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34461
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34461
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34461]] Task Relevance-Driven Prompt Expansion for Federated Continual Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34461)
  • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Federated continual learning (FCL) has attracted considerable interest by enabling local clients to continually acquire knowledge from new tasks. However, most FCL methods assume that the global server has prior knowledge of the arrival times and number of categories for new tasks, which is unrealistic in the real world. Furthermore, they neglect the importance of positive inter-task semantic relationships when learning new tasks and are significantly affected by irrelevant relationships from old tasks, thereby exacerbating catastrophic forgetting. To resolve the above challenges, we develop a novel Task Relevance-driven Prompt Expansion (TRPE) model. Specifically, to eliminate the reliance on prior knowledge of new tasks, we propose an out-of-distribution detector to identify the arrival of new tasks within local clients, along with a radius-adaptive DBSCAN to infer the quantity of categories in new tasks on the proxy server. Moreover, to explore positive inter-task relationships, we calculate the affinity between the original gradient for learning new task and its gradient orthogonal to the feature space of old tasks to evaluate semantic relevance between old and new tasks from a global perspective. Then, we use this global task relevance to determine the key-prompt pair for new tasks and identify highly related old tasks. After concatenating their prompts for training, we can leverage such positive inter-task relations to improve the continual learning of newly arriving tasks. To balance the learning of new tasks and the forgetting of old ones, a soft orthogonality constraint is applied to the local key and prompt gradients. Experiments spanning diverse datasets and backbones confirm the efficacy of our TRPE model.

Title: Finite-Sample Sensitivity of Kernel Metrics for Generative Evaluation

  • Authors: Tomasz Radzikowski, Mateusz Modrzejewski
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.SD
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34477
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34477
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34477]] Finite-Sample Sensitivity of Kernel Metrics for Generative Evaluation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34477)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Kernel distances such as the Kernel Inception Distance (KID) and the Kernel Audio Distance (KAD) compare generated and reference samples in an embedding space, and model comparisons rest on their values. Each ships with one fixed kernel, and a reported value does not say which changes that kernel detects at the sample size used. We run each default as a permutation two-sample test on real embeddings of symbolic music, audio and images, comparing a sample with an independent sample changed by a mean shift, an isotropic contraction, or a rotation that keeps the mean and the covariance trace and is matched to the shift in transport distance. At 780 samples per side, both defaults rarely detect the rotations in five evaluation spaces, while they detect the shifts in every run. The median-bandwidth Gaussian kernel of KAD detects the contraction and regains power against rotations on image embeddings with an order of magnitude more samples. The cubic kernel of KID contains second- and third-moment information but misses the contraction in most runs, and our experiments do not establish a high-power rotation regime for it over the tested sample sizes. Narrower Gaussian bandwidths restore sensitivity in music and audio, an $\ell_1$ Laplace kernel does so for images, and aggregated tests combine both at nominal level. We recommend that a reported kernel distance state its sample budget and the changes its configuration detects at that budget.

  • Keywords: defense, attack, robust, large language model

Existing agent-safety benchmarks measure generic harm or prompt-injection success and implicitly treat refusing as the safe default. In public-safety deployments that assumption breaks: an agent too cautious to dispatch help fails as surely as one that triggers a false alert. We present SentryBench, which scores agents on a two-sided, cost-sensitive frontier jointly measuring attack success, over-refusal (missing a real emergency), and over-reaction (unwarranted action) under adversarial tool use, with scenarios grounded in real emergency-call transcripts, disaster messages, and real injection/jailbreak payloads. We formalize deployment harm as an asymmetric cost functional, prove naive caution is harm-dominated whenever it refuses more genuine emergencies than the attack harm it averts, and prove a targeted trust gate adds zero over-refusal on clean input. Across 840 scored episodes over three frontier models and three defenses, refuse-more caution drives over-refusal to 100 percent on two of three models for at most a 3.7-point change in attack success and yields the worst harm everywhere, whereas our trust-gating wrapper dominates the frontier (lowest harm on every model, over-refusal at or below baseline, higher containment). Provider content filters also silently perform part of the defending and must be reported separately.

Title: STEP: Soft Trajectory Estimation of Prediction Correctness via Per-Step Probability Scoring

  • Authors: Chenyang Yu, Zifan Zeng, Qunli Zhang, Feng Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34537
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34537
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34537]] STEP: Soft Trajectory Estimation of Prediction Correctness via Per-Step Probability Scoring(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34537)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Estimating the correctness of large language model (LLM) predictions without access to ground truth is critical for reliable deployment in high-stakes settings. Existing approaches, such as entropy and perplexity, operate solely on final output statistics, discarding the rich information encoded in intermediate reasoning steps. To address this limitation, we propose STEP (Soft Trajectory Estimation of Prediction correctness), a training-free framework that scores each reasoning step via lightweight forward passes to obtain a soft probability distribution qi over candidate answers, from which we derive a set of soft trajectory features capturing per-step confidence and its progression toward the final answer. Extensive experiments on various benchmarks demonstrate consistent and substantial improvements, particularly in AUROC, over output-based baselines and hard-indicator trajectory methods. Notably, Temporally-Weighted Consistency, which assigns higher importance to later reasoning steps where answer distributions have stabilized, contributes the largest gains, highlighting the importance of temporal structure in reasoning trajectories. These results further validate the advantage of preserving continuous probability signals over binary discretization.

Title: LLM WikiLeaks: Uncovering URL-Based Capability Extension and Security Circumvention in Agentic Message Boards

  • Authors: Orel Navi, Elad Feldman, Ben Nassi
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34542
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34542
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34542]] LLM WikiLeaks: Uncovering URL-Based Capability Extension and Security Circumvention in Agentic Message Boards(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34542)
  • Keywords: security, extraction
  • Abstract: Autonomous agents have been observed acting beyond their assigned tasks, accessing unauthorized systems, and establishing communication channels to exchange information, yet the Web and network-level techniques they use to extend their capabilities and circumvent security restrictions remain poorly understood. We study these techniques through the ProWiki incident, in which agents restricted to outbound HTTP GET requests repurposed a public German-language wiki as a message board. We analyze 14,591 agent edits containing 79,541 URL postings and present corroborating evidence that the published URLs reflect the agents' operational activity. We find that 26,717 URL occurrences (33.6%) embed target URLs within one to five layers of third-party Web services. By recursively reconstructing these nested URLs, we identify 67 distinct intermediate services and classify their functionality. Most intermediate layers (73.8%) extend agents' capabilities by delegating content extraction and transformation to external Web services: Markdown and OCR services convert web content into machine-readable text, JSON filtering services extract selected fields, and archival services expose historical versions of pages. The remaining 26.2% facilitate circumvention of access restrictions and evasion of detection, including CORS and fetch proxies that retrieve content on agents' behalf, and link shorteners and redirectors that hide destination URLs from mechanisms inspecting literal URLs or outer hostnames. Agents also reach the same targets through multiple URL paths, consistent with attempts to evade rate limits or obscure request origins. Our results expose a gap between the capabilities granted to agents and those they can obtain by composing publicly available Web services: restricting an agent's local toolset or direct Internet access may be insufficient while third-party services remain reachable.

Title: CVM-Store: A Secure Storage System for Confidential Virtual Machines

  • Authors: Thore Sommer, Masanori Misono, Dimitrios Stavrakakis, Pramod Bhatotia
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34560
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34560
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34560]] CVM-Store: A Secure Storage System for Confidential Virtual Machines(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34560)
  • Keywords: secure, security, protect
  • Abstract: Stateful cloud workloads require high-performance persistent storage when running on untrusted cloud infrastructure. Confidential Virtual Machines (CVMs) promise to protect these workloads through hardware-enforced memory encryption and isolation without requiring application changes. However, they protect only data in use; persistent storage lies outside the CVM trust boundary, exposing all stateful data to the cloud provider. Extending the trust boundary to persistent storage presents inherent challenges. High-bandwidth workloads require near-raw NVMe throughput, yet the default kernel CVM I/O stacks are inadequate, and userspace I/O libraries are not designed with CVM use cases in mind. Access patterns and storage semantics vary between workloads, necessitating access-pattern-aware security abstractions rather than uniform block-level encryption. In order to mitigate against rollback, freshness is needed, but CVMs lack hardware primitives for this purpose, requiring alternative solutions. We present CVM-Store, a storage system that co-designs a high-throughput userspace I/O engine with CIF (confidentiality, integrity, freshness) data protection. The key insight is that neither authenticated encryption nor I/O is a bottleneck by itself, but their naive combination is. CVM-Store exploits this by interleaving them, eliminating redundant copies and OS/hypervisor mediation. CIF guarantees are provided as composable primitives that adapt protection to workload access patterns, from which we derive three concrete authenticated abstractions: a KVS, a log, and a filesystem. An asynchronous trusted counter service provides freshness guarantees with epoch-based batching, which enables rollback protection without dedicated hardware support. CVM-Store delivers full CIF guarantees within $\approx$1% (write) and 1-19% (read) of raw NVMe bandwidth for large objects.

Title: Recursive CLIP: Vision-Language Distillation with Looped Transformer Blocks

  • Authors: Szymon Mazurek, Alberto Presta, Michał Byra
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34563
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34563
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34563]] Recursive CLIP: Vision-Language Distillation with Looped Transformer Blocks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34563)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Contrastive vision-language models such as CLIP typically rely on independently parameterized transformer blocks across depth. We investigate the extent to which these blocks can instead be reused recurrently while preserving the transferable capabilities of CLIP. We study recursive transformer backbones in which a small set of $N$ transformer blocks is applied $M$ times per forward pass. By holding $N \cdot M$ fixed to the depth of a standard ViT-B, our models preserve computational depth while progressively increasing parameter sharing. We consider two recursive CLIP model families: rCLIP, where only the vision encoder is recursive, and r$^2$CLIP, where recurrence is also introduced in the text encoder. We study these models under both direct contrastive training and through knowledge distillation from a strong ViT-B/16 teacher pretrained on DFN-2B, using the substantially smaller CC3M and CC12M datasets. We evaluate the resulting models on zero-shot classification and cross-modal retrieval, and further study their adaptability through few-shot learning, visual grounding, and concept unlearning. Our results show that CLIP can tolerate substantial recurrent block reuse, although its effect depends on the encoder and downstream task. Recurrence also provides parameter efficiency by construction, since fewer distinct transformer blocks need to be stored and adapted. Our results establish recurrent block reuse as a practical design space for vision-language models and highlight its implications for knowledge transfer, downstream adaptation, and parameter efficiency.

Title: Error Diffusion Replaces Latent Weights: Training Binary-Weight Language Models on a Few Bits of Dithered State

  • Authors: Pierre-Olivier Bonin
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34566
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34566
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34566]] Error Diffusion Replaces Latent Weights: Training Binary-Weight Language Models on a Few Bits of Dithered State(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34566)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Binary-weight language models store one bit per weight, but training them keeps a full-precision latent copy of each weight plus Adam state, about 96 extra bits per weight, so a deployed binary model cannot be adapted on the device that runs it. We replace the latent weight with a 2-8-bit residual buffer quantized by stochastic rounding, which cuts the training state per weight from 97 bits to 3-9, up to 19x less. The resulting error-diffusion (ED) optimizer moves the residual-carrying loop of image halftoning onto the weight-update path. Each buffer accumulates the unrealized part of every update and flips the weight when the accumulated value crosses a threshold, and it works only when its quantization grid has a level exactly at that threshold. Under this threshold-alignment rule, 4-bit buffers match 8-bit buffers on a binary multilayer perceptron benchmark, and the same rule lifts a different latent-free optimizer, Bop, from a 10-point deficit at 4 bits to full-precision accuracy. On character-level transformers with 97% of parameters binary, ED trains within 5-11% of the latent-weight reference at a 2,000-step budget and within 1.7% at convergence. On scaling ladders over enwik8 and wikitext-103, up to 19M parameters on enwik8 (70M with the wikitext-103 embeddings), the latent-free loss stays within -2.3% to +2.9% of the reference at all six sizes once a per-step cap on the buffer update removes a launch instability; without the cap it trails by 10% at the largest tokenized size. A deployed binary model continues training under ED at one tenth of the latent training state, matching latent-weight continuation, and a fused CPU kernel completes the adaptation within a 275 MB training-memory budget, which latent-weight training cannot. A 250-step probe sets the flip learning rate at any new scale.

  • Abstract: Autonomous underwater vehicles (AUVs) navigate without satellite positioning and rely on Doppler velocity log (DVL) and inertial sensors for motion estimation. To overcome this limitation, we present a conditional diffusion framework that generates synthetic DVL and inertial signals to replace real recordings in training. The generator employs an Elucidated diffusion model conditioned on three per-channel motion descriptors. Title: Cumulative Marginal Matching for One-Step Diffusion

  • Authors: Paribesh Regmi, Rui Li
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34575
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34575
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34575]] Cumulative Marginal Matching for One-Step Diffusion(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34575)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: The sampling procedures of diffusion and flow-matching models involve hundreds of denoising steps incurring substantial inference time, which has motivated a growing body of work on training schemes for few-step and one-step generation. Existing approaches primarily seek to align the generation trajectory of few-step and base models, yet their training objectives only involve discrepancies at a single point in the trajectory, encouraging only local \emph{point-level} alignment. While recent methods such as \emph{Shortcut-CSL} introduce cumulative objective that enforce alignment over the entire trajectory, this is suboptimal as they do not directly enforce alignment between the distributions induced by the two models throughout the generation process. We propose \emph{Cumulative Marginal Matching} (CMM), a principled objective for few-step generation: by viewing flow-matching as a sequence of intermediate marginal distributions connecting the prior and data distributions, CMM directly optimizes the cumulative discrepancy between the base and few-step marginal distributions over the entire sequence. Specifically, we formulate this discrepancy using the Wasserstein-2 distance. By exploiting the pushforward representation of flow-matching, we transform an intractable cumulative Wasserstein objective into a tractable surrogate that can be optimized using samples from the intermediate marginal, which are readily generated through the conditional interpolation process of flow-matching. Furthermore, we show that the existing \emph{Shortcut-CSL} objective arises as a first-order approximation of that of the CMM, establishing a theoretical connection between the two objectives. Experiments on standard benchmarks demonstrate improvement in generation quality over \emph{Shortcut-CSL} and other state-of-the-art one- and few-step baselines under identical training budgets.

  • Abstract: Diffusion models can sometimes reproduce examples from their training data, a phenomenon known as memorization. Existing memorization detection methods for unconditional diffusion models typically operate at a pre-selected timestep and therefore cannot adapt the detection time to the evolving denoising trajectory. Sequential monitoring enables adaptive detection but repeated testing across timesteps complicates trajectory-level false-alarm control. We study two ways of combining split conformal calibration with sequential monitoring for prompt-free memorization detection: per-step Bonferroni calibration and direct trajectory-level calibration of the maximum memorization score. The former controls repeated testing by splitting the false-alarm budget across timesteps, whereas the latter calibrates the event of ever crossing a threshold directly through the trajectory-level maximum. We evaluate this formulation on an unconditional CIFAR-10 EDM using two complementary per-step statistics based on geometry and representation spikiness. Experiments show that sequential monitoring improves the power-timing tradeoff over fixed-time detection. Compared with Bonferroni calibration, trajectory-level calibration achieves comparable detection power while using the false-alarm budget less conservatively and enabling earlier detection at higher noise levels.

To bridge this gap, we propose P2P-Former, a novel point-to-proposal Transformer-based framework that effectively connects point-level supervision with proposal-level learning for temporal action localization. In this work, we identify two key challenges in transitioning from point-level supervision to full supervision: (1) how to generate reliable proposals as pseudo ground-truth, and (2) how to effectively integrate point-level cues into the fully supervised paradigm. To address the first challenge, we propose a Point-aware Proposal Generation (PPG) strategy that refines the confidence of proposals produced by a base locator using a point-aware importance estimation function, and then performs optimal matching between point annotations and candidate proposals to select reliable proposals as pseudo ground-truth. To tackle the second challenge, we introduce a Point-guided Semantic Enhancement (PSE) module that aggregates prototypes from point-annotated features and injects them into snippet features through Transformer-based interactions. Moreover, we design a Point-to-Proposal Constraint Loss (PPCL) that enforces each predicted proposal to include the annotated point, thereby encouraging more accurate localization. Extensive experiments on the THUMOS14 and ActivityNet-1.3 datasets demonstrate that our method outperforms several existing state-of-the-art approaches.

This paper investigates decision models as an alternative to generative LLMs and fine-tuned PLMs for entity matching.   • Authors: Kevin David Hayes, Alex Hanson, Allen Tu, Pranav Asthana, Matthias Zwicker, Tom Goldstein
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34632
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34632
