  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Driving video captured through a windshield violates the world-space consistency assumed by 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS): the road scene exhibits parallax while cabin reflections remain attached to the camera, causing standard 3DGS to embed reflected content into the reconstructed scene. We introduce RIDE-Splat, a pipeline for reflection-free 3D reconstruction from posed driving video without clean images, reflection masks, or pretrained networks. RIDE-Splat assigns world-consistent transmission through the windshield to a 3DGS scene and the dominant camera-attached reflection to one shared, signed RGB image. It builds the clean reconstruction in two passes. First, it learns the 3D geometry and the signed reflection image, representing both added radiance and transmission attenuation. Then, it uses the reflection-removed training images to fit a clean 3DGS model. At inference, the reflection image is discarded, preserving the standard 3DGS rendering path. We also introduce the RIDE-Splat Dataset, the first 3D reconstruction benchmark for reflection removal from driving video -- with real driving scenes, physical windshield reflections in training views, and separately captured non-reflective evaluation images. Across the RIDE-Splat Dataset, XLD, and Wayve, RIDE-Splat outperforms 3DGS, DC-Gaussian, and RobustSplat on all nine aggregate metrics, improving PSNR by up to 3.59 dB and reducing LPIPS by up to 0.0876. We achieve the highest metrics in 38 out of 42 per-scene comparisons and removes windshield reflections that remain visible in the baselines while preserving the transmitted scene.

Title: Video World Models Need Predictable Latents

  • Authors: Mattia Scardecchia, Sherry Yang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CV, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34663
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34663
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34663]] Video World Models Need Predictable Latents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34663)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Video world models trained on large-scale robot data promise a safe and scalable approach for learning and evaluating policies that act in the physical world. However, they still struggle with long-horizon rollouts and out-of-distribution generalization, and they remain computationally demanding at inference time. Typically, video world models make predictions in the latent space of a reconstruction-trained autoencoder, which however is not directly optimized for dynamics modeling. On the other hand, world models designed for latent planning usually optimize latents to be predictable, but they forgo accurate prediction of observations in pixel space. In this paper, we show that it is possible to achieve the best of both worlds: a latent geometry optimized to make future prediction as simple as possible without sacrificing accurate prediction of future observations. More precisely, we find that temporal objectives for representation learning, paired with a past-conditioned decoder architecture, lead to consistent improvements in generative video world models at the same training budget. The gains include up to 5x cheaper inference, over 7x faster training, and 15-24% lower pixel and perceptual errors out of distribution and over long horizons. Our world models also agree more closely with real-world policy evaluation when using a vision-language model to judge task success from their pixel-space predictions. Overall, our study indicates that temporal predictability is an important design criterion for the latent space of video world models, and provides concrete guidance on how to incorporate it into the training of autoencoders.

However, parameter-based fingerprints can be sensitive to model transformations, limiting their robustness, while representation-based fingerprints may assign high similarity to unrelated models, limiting their uniqueness. Title: Exploring perceptual straightness in learned visual representations

  • Authors: Anne Harrington, Vasha DuTell, Ayush Tewari, Mark Hamilton, Simon Stent, Ruth Rosenholtz, William T. Freeman
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34678
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34678
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34678]] Exploring perceptual straightness in learned visual representations(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34678)
  • Keywords: robust, transformer, segmentation
  • Abstract: Humans have been shown to use a "straightened" encoding to represent the natural visual world as it evolves in time (Hénaff et al. 2019). In the context of discrete video sequences, "straightened" means that changes between frames follow a more linear path in representation space at progressively deeper levels of processing. While deep convolutional networks are often proposed as models of human visual processing, many do not straighten natural videos. In this paper, we explore the relationship between network architecture, differing types of robustness, biologically-inspired filtering mechanisms, and representational straightness in response to time-varying input; we identify strengths and limitations of straightness as a useful way of evaluating neural network representations. We find that (1) adversarial training leads to straighter representations in both CNN and transformer-based architectures but (2) this effect is task-dependent, not generalizing to tasks such as segmentation and frame-prediction, where straight representations are not favorable for predictions; and nor to other types of robustness. In addition, (3) straighter representations impart temporal stability to class predictions, even for out-of-distribution data. Finally, (4) biologically-inspired elements increase straightness in the early stages of a network, but do not guarantee increased straightness in downstream layers of CNNs. We show that straightness is an easily computed measure of representational robustness and stability, as well as a hallmark of human representations with benefits for computer vision models.

Separately, we identify two sources of target performance leakage in the prior ranking-based evaluation protocol and provide corrections that remove both.

Experiments across three base models and two benchmarks demonstrate that our early reduction accelerates inference significantly and mitigates peak GPU memory. Furthermore, we can improve generation quality by safely filtering out noisy, uninformative reference tokens, enabling the model to concentrate its limited attention capacity on genuinely useful tokens. Title: Natural Jammers: When Non-Robust Features Become Antagonistic

  • Authors: Jiaming Zhang, Fuyao Zhang, Pengjun Xie, Wei Yang Bryan Lim
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34785
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34785
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34785]] Natural Jammers: When Non-Robust Features Become Antagonistic(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34785)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Deep neural networks are known to rely on non-robust features that align poorly with human perception. Prior work has largely framed these features as opportunistic shortcuts that help standard models on clean data. We show instead that non-robust cues can be antagonistic: they can act as Natural Jammers that actively mislead standard models on clean, unmodified images. Using disagreement between a standard model and robust models as an analysis lens, we identify a Jammed Set on which robust models outperform standard models. We provide causal evidence for Spectral-spatial Locking: jamming arises only when high-frequency components are precisely aligned with specific spatial structures. Disrupting this lock through spectral filtering or small geometric shifts can restore correct predictions. Crucially, we uncover a Substitution Paradox: while individual jammers are fragile to geometric shifts, the aggregate prevalence of jamming remains approximately stable and is redistributed rather than eliminated under such transformations. Our results reframe natural non-robustness as a failure field over the empirical model-data geometry: one that redistributes rather than disappears under small transformations.

Title: Phantom Forgetting: How BatchNorm State Fakes Deletion in Edge Split Machine Unlearning

  • Authors: Anand Agrawal, Anil Kumar Chillara, Naresh Mandan
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34800
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34800
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34800]] Phantom Forgetting: How BatchNorm State Fakes Deletion in Edge Split Machine Unlearning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34800)
  • Keywords: security, privacy
  • Abstract: Split learning is a natural fit for edge intelligence: a resource-constrained edge device runs the first layers of a network and offloads the remainder to a server, so that raw data never leave the device. When such a system must unlearn revoked or sensitive data for example to honor a right-to-erasure request, the deletion is commonly assessed through changes in forget-set accuracy. However, inference on an edge-server split also depends on non-learnable model state distributed across the two parties, and this state is especially volatile on edge devices subject to data drift, over-the-air updates, and checkpoint rollback. This paper isolates a state-integrity question with direct edge-security implications: can Batch Normalization (BN) running statistics create a target-specific forgetting signal even when every learnable weight remains fixed? We define a state-only diagnostic that separates learnable-parameter change from BN- buffer change and localizes the effect to the edge (client) side, the server side, and joint placements. We also introduce a state-induced forgetting gap (SIFG) that discounts simultaneous retain-set degradation. Over all ten CIFAR-10 forget classes, centralized BN recalibration on forget data reduces mean forget accuracy by 61.50 percentage points with zero weight change; in a split model, edge-only, server-only, and joint recalibration produce mean drops of 37.27, 41.71, and 51.90 points. The server-side state yields the largest selective gap relative to retain degradation, while joint recalibration causes the largest overall prediction change. These results establish distributed normalization buffers including those held on the edge device as security and privacy-relevant inference state, and show that forget accuracy should not be interpreted, or accepted as a deletion certificate, without an explicit edge-aware state-integrity audit.

  • Abstract: Language models now answer questions alongside other claims: a note in the system prompt, a message from another agent, their own earlier answer. Second, attributing the note to an authority rather than to another participant, and phrasing it assertively, each raise error by 10-27 points, while the two authorities we tested differ by under two. Third, replaying a correct worked answer before the advice protects it whether the text is shown as the model's own or as someone else's, and the same replay keeps wrong answers from being corrected by a bare correct note.   • Abstract: Diffusion MRI (dMRI) provides information about white matter microstructure that can support brain age prediction. Deep learning is increasingly used for brain age prediction, but conventional CNNs do not explicitly respect the geometric structure of orientation-dependent features. Our results indicated that the performance advantage depended on combining WM-FODs with equivariant processing. Prediction accuracy was highest at a maximum SH order of Lmax = 2 and declined at higher orders. Tract-level occlusion, where each segmented tract was masked in turn, revealed age-dependent patterns of model sensitivity, with importance peaks in frontal projection pathways at 36-44 years, the fornix at 45-54 years, the callosal isthmus at 55-64 years, the middle cerebellar peduncle at 65-74 years, and the callosal genu at 75-90 years. These findings support rotation-equivariant processing of WM-FODs for accurate brain age prediction with anatomically interpretable model sensitivity.

  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: LLM post-training often optimizes multiple objectives with unequal priorities, such as correctness and reasoning efficiency. Experiments on multiple domains and models show that PAPO consistently achieves the best primary objective while maintaining best or competitive auxiliary-objective performance across both GRPO- and PPO-based optimization. \href{this https URL}{Code is available}.

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34915

  • Abstract: Low-precision arithmetic can make reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models cheaper, but RL requires the sampler and the learner to agree. Without a quantized KV cache, BF16 end to end remains 2.5-10x more consistent. (2) Speed. MXFP8 training adds a fixed cost per micro-batch and saves a per-token cost, so in our dense setting it pays off only above about 4.5k tokens per micro-batch; a micro-batch sweep on identical data confirms the per-micro-batch cost, and a trace shows the GPU waiting for the host for most of the added time. End to end, MXFP8 cuts MoE step time by 10.7% and generation time by 24%, and slows a short-sequence dense workload by 5%. (3) Correctness. We state seven conditions every weight synchronization must preserve and checks that test them against the live engine. Each of the seven failures we met on hardware broke one condition, five silently; the checks caught two at their first opportunity, each missed by the other checks, and CPU fault-injection tests reproduce five. All runs are single-seed and at most 60 steps long; we make no claim about final model quality.

Title: Attack logics, not outputs: Concept-based Property Attacks for Shortcut Elimination in Neural Networks

  • Authors: Raik Dankworth, Felix Mächtle, Thomas Eisenbarth, Gesina Schwalbe
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34923
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34923
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34923]] Attack logics, not outputs: Concept-based Property Attacks for Shortcut Elimination in Neural Networks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34923)
  • Keywords: security, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Deep neural networks (NNs) for computer vision are vulnerable to adversarial attacks, such that minuscule malicious changes to inputs may induce unintuitive outputs. One key approach to verify and mitigate such robustness issues is to falsify expected output behavior, which enables local robustness guarantees and adversarial training. Due to the black-box nature of NNs, current attacks only falsify a class of the final output, such as flipping from $\text{\texttt{stop_sign}}$ to $\neg \text{\texttt{stop_sign}}$. In this work we argue that robustness analysis should focus more generally on logical inconsistencies in concept-level reasoning rather than solely on output misclassification. We formalize this perspective through concept-based properties, i.e., constraints over human-interpretable concepts such as $\text{\texttt{red}} \land \text{\texttt{octogonal}} \implies \text{\texttt{stop_sign}}$ and study the falsification of such properties as a principled form of adversarial attack. Furthermore, an implementation of concept-based properties on already trained NNs is proposed using techniques from concept-based explainable artificial intelligence (C-XAI). For that we formally prove that attacks on concept-based properties are expected to have a reduced search space compared to simple class falsification, whilst arguably be more aligned with intuitive robustness targets. The practical effectiveness of our approach for logically informed adversarial training is shown in an empirical study across multiple models and datasets, including both vision and a security-relevant code processing task. Our results demonstrate that concept-level falsification generalizes classical adversarial attacks: Retraining on our novel attacks maintains robustness gains as compared to classical adversarial retraining whilst better eliminating semantic biases.

Title: Relational Composition as Parallel Transport: What Per-Entity Frames Force in Attention

  • Authors: Aakash Sarkar, Ananya Mukherjee, Motoshige Sato
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.FL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34945
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34945
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34945]] Relational Composition as Parallel Transport: What Per-Entity Frames Force in Attention(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34945)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: A dot product means something only when both vectors share a coordinate system. Content words have one; a fresh entity does not: rotating its coordinates alone changes nothing. We take this per-entity frame freedom as a symmetry and ask what it forces in attention. For orthogonal transports, invariant scores depend only on norms and the transported inner product $\langle x_i,T_{ij}x_j\rangle$; equivariant messages lie in the span of $x_i$ and $T_{ij}x_j$. Endpoint channel count bounds transport rank, so with one vector per entity relations must be supplied; standard attention is the flat sector of a shared frame. Representation theory attaches an integer to each mechanism; the readout decides which. With orthogonal transitions, a convex-cell readout composes a finite group exactly only at or above its smallest faithful real representation dimension. At this minimum, exactness forces a faithful representation. An even readout can decode a cover at smaller width, and the gap is unbounded. Below its threshold, the part of the group a mechanism cannot see sets an accuracy ceiling computable before training. 17/17 fitting seeds at minimal width passed tests through length 1000, versus 7/18 at twice the width; 12 models carry correctness certificates to explicit lengths, from the weights alone. On $C_3\times A_4$ (faithful width 5), descent at width 4 learned the 72-state double cover under sign-blind attention (6/48 seeds; monotone 0/48); on a frozen cover the cross-entropy-optimal affine head is uniform, a quadratic head exact. On parsed CLUTRR, transported composition beats a matched transformer by 41 points at 4-10 hops, but forcing isometries costs 65 points; on StepGame's commutative algebra, isometries help. Static embeddings gave each word one vector; contextual models gave each occurrence its own; per-entity frames make each relation an operator, the unit in which relations compose.

Title: The Target Agent Is Its Own Best Critic but a Poor Proposer: Self-Improved Red Teaming

  • Authors: Mintong Kang, Stephen Rawls, Melissa Kazemi Rad, Jian Ni, Bo Li
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34965
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34965
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34965]] The Target Agent Is Its Own Best Critic but a Poor Proposer: Self-Improved Red Teaming(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34965)
  • Keywords: attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Automatic red teaming increasingly relies on large language models (LLMs) to iteratively diagnose failed attacks and propose improved adversarial prompts or injections. Although prior work strengthens this loop through prompt engineering and search-harness design, the mechanisms underlying its two core roles, criticism and attack proposal, has not been explored. To further investigate the core roles and therefore improve red teaming effectiveness, we disentangle two design choices: whether criticism is generated by the target session itself (self-criticism) or by a separately instantiated critic (external criticism), and whether that criticism is translated into the next attack by the target session (self-proposal) or by a separate attacker (external proposal). Through our analysis, we find that self-criticism is more effective because it is grounded in the target's live execution context, including observations, constraints, and available reasoning; removing reasoning reduces downstream attack success by 6%-17%. In contrast, self-proposal is substantially less effective because awareness of the adversarial objective induces explicit refusal and non-refusal sandbagging. These findings motivate a role-separated red-teaming approach: the target session diagnoses why an attack failed, while a separately instantiated attacker generates the next proposal. On DTAP-BENCH, spanning Telecom, Workflow, CRM, and Code domains, under both direct misuse and indirect threat models, as well as multiple agent frameworks, this configuration achieves 63.2% overall attack success rate (ASR), much higher than baselines. Our results show that effective automatic red teaming depends not only on model capability and search strategy, but also on how execution context and adversarial responsibility are distributed across model sessions.

Title: SVD-Attention: Efficiently Analyzing Attention Blocks in Transformers

  • Authors: Debopam Sanyal, Hongjie Chen, Riya Elizabeth Kuriyan, Arth Tak, Divya Kothandaraman, Akshay Mehra, Joshua Kimball, Alexey Tumanov
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34971
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34971
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34971]] SVD-Attention: Efficiently Analyzing Attention Blocks in Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34971)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Pretrained transformers spend a large share of their inference compute inside self-attention, yet the per-head query-key interaction is structurally low-rank by construction. We ask a diagnostic question: across layers and architectures, which heads actually use the full rank budget on real data, and which do not? We propose SVD-Attention, a post-hoc method that decomposes the per-head interaction matrix $M_h$ and scores every singular mode by both its spectral magnitude and its activation variance across tokens on a small calibration set. The score keeps the modes the head can amplify and that real data actually activates, and discards the rest; the resulting factorization is exact for the retained modes and needs no finetuning. Across seven pretrained models and five task types, the diagnostic finds a task-dependent pattern: redundancy sits in the early to middle layers for sentiment and image classification and for generation, and in the late layers for dense prediction and extractive question answering. It is also predictive: truncating the layers it flags costs at most $1.75$ F1, while truncating the same number of layers elsewhere in the same model costs up to $50$ F1. SVD-Attention is an analysis tool rather than a compression method: acting on it reduces inference FLOPs by $0.8$-$1.5\%$ on its own and by $8$-$11\%$ when composed with weight pruning, with $0$-$4\%$ change in task quality.

Title: Adaptive Memory Index Evolution for Agents

  • Authors: Yuntong Hu, Tongli Su, Yuanfu Sun, Shuo Ma, Qiaoyu Tan, Liang Zhao
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34977
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34977
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.34977]] Adaptive Memory Index Evolution for Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34977)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Long-horizon agents continually accumulate experience, yet retaining a memory does not guarantee access when it is needed. Existing systems can adapt both memory content and access; we isolate persistent access updates as a separately controlled adaptation layer. We formulate this target as Memory Index Evolution and instantiate it with Adaptive Memory Index Evolution (AMIE), a lightweight plug-in layer that evolves per-memory access state from deployment retrievals without modifying stored content or retraining the host memory system. Controlled analyses show that persistent competitor suppression can expose previously unseen evidence, while suppressing repeatedly useful memories creates the complementary failure mode. Together, these results establish persistent memory access as a practical adaptation layer, distinct from the content it serves, in retrieval-based agent memory systems.

Title: Attention under Bounded Key-Query Similarity: Space Complexity and Transformer Anisotropy

  • Authors: Justin Y. Chen, Ying Feng, Piotr Indyk, Michael Kapralov, Ekaterina Kochetkova, Boris Prokhorov
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.DS
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35020
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35020
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35020]] Attention under Bounded Key-Query Similarity: Space Complexity and Transformer Anisotropy(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35020)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: A growing body of work on KV-cache compression aims to reduce the memory requirements of autoregressive generation. These methods compress the stored context while approximately preserving attention computation, enabling generation with reduced memory consumption. Almost tight theoretical results on the worst case space complexity of attention have recently been obtained, but they are achieved by instances where queries can be perfectly aligned with keys. This directly contradicts the observed anisotropy of the key/query geometry in real transformers. In particular, it has been shown empirically that key/query cosine similarities are small even for most correlated pairs, i.e., near-perfect alignment of queries with keys does not happen in practice. In this paper, we study the space complexity of attention approximation under the assumption that key/query cosine similarities belong to a small interval around zero, i.e., a query cannot align too much with any key. We give a nearly space optimal algorithm and a corresponding space lower bound. Surprisingly, the distribution of key/query cosine similarities in our lower bound exhibits the anisotropy property empirically established and studied in the literature. Additionally, we show that the simple uniform sampling algorithm is nearly optimal for a typical range of parameters, supporting its surprising effectiveness on real datasets.

Title: BellowsKV: One Latent for Many Layers, Expanded on Demand

  • Authors: Jiamu Zhang, Hanjie Chen
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35023
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35023
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35023]] BellowsKV: One Latent for Many Layers, Expanded on Demand(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35023)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) now serve as the backbone of agents that reason over many turns, call tools, and pursue long-horizon tasks. To avoid recomputation, a model stores the attention keys and values of all past tokens in a key-value (KV) cache. Each layer maintains its own cache, so KV-cache memory can become the main bottleneck for GPU serving. Most compression methods operate within a single layer, by quantizing the cache entries, reducing the channel dimension, or evicting less important tokens. All three lose substantial accuracy at high compression ratios. Cross-layer methods share one compressed cache across adjacent layers to go beyond this limit. However, these methods also degrade sharply at high compression ratios. We find that the KV-cache representations of adjacent layers are quite different, whereas the input hidden states of adjacent layers are highly similar. We also find that, under the same memory budget, reducing the dimension of this shared latent loses far more accuracy than quantizing it. Based on these findings, we propose BellowsKV, a training-free method that maps the hidden states of n adjacent layers to one shared latent per token. At inference time, each layer reads its own keys and values from this latent through a layer-specific projection. We keep the latent at full dimension and compress it in two other ways: BellowsKV-Quant stores the latent at low precision, which is nearly lossless because the latent has no dominant outlier channels; BellowsKV-Evict makes one shared eviction decision per block, because the layers rank token importance very similarly. Across Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B and Qwen2.5-7B, BellowsKV-Quant retains about 99% of full-cache accuracy at 3.6x compression and 97-98% at 13-14x, where the strongest cross-layer method retains at most 84% at 16x. Title: Reinforcement Learning Done Right for Explicit Confidence Targets

  • Authors: Zefeng He, Yichen He, Yuan Lin, Yang Yang, Yu Cheng
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35059
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35059
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35059]] Reinforcement Learning Done Right for Explicit Confidence Targets(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35059)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can produce plausible but incorrect answers when they are uncertain, which can mislead users and undermine model reliability. Training models to abstain with reinforcement learning is a natural approach, but most existing methods use a fixed error penalty and therefore cannot adapt their behavior to different confidence requirements at inference time. We study \emph{explicit confidence targets}, where a user specifies a target \(p\) in the prompt and the model directly chooses whether to answer or abstain, with the goal of achieving an accuracy of at least \(p\) among answered questions. However, learning to respond to different confidence targets with a single model has proven difficult, and prior attempts have failed to distinguish different targets. We show that this failure comes from two problems: the reward signal that distinguishes different values of $p$ is often lost during optimization, while the original error penalty grows without bound as $p$ approaches one. We propose RLEC (\textbf{R}einforcement \textbf{L}earning with \textbf{E}xplicit \textbf{C}onfidence Targets), which preserves the learning signal needed to distinguish confidence targets, introduces a stable reward family that keeps the desired answer condition unchanged, and uses reward scheduling to balance effective learning and training stability. Together, these designs enable the first successful training of a single LLM for explicit confidence target control. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks and model scales show that RLEC largely preserves general capabilities and achieves competitive calibration performance. RLEC also supports fine-grained target control without an external decision system or threshold calibration after training. By making explicit confidence targets part of the trained policy, RLEC opens a new and more direct path toward behavioral calibration.

In particular, coordinated attacks can introduce multiple mutually reinforcing poisoned documents into retrieved contexts, making reliable evidence filtering challenging. To address this problem, we propose GuardRAG, a training-free and plug-and-play post-retrieval defense that exploits the semantic structure of retrieved evidence to identify suspicious document groups. Specifically, GuardRAG first expands the retrieval range and removes near-duplicate candidates, then partitions the retained documents using Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering (HAC) with a query-adaptive threshold. Finally, it scores and prunes suspicious clusters based on query relevance, cluster size, and semantic compactness. Experiments across three question-answering datasets, multiple poisoning attacks, and six LLMs demonstrate that GuardRAG substantially reduces attack success rates while maintaining answer utility under clean retrieval.

Title: CoSA: The Conflict of Skills Attack in Real-World LLM Agent Ecosystems

  • Authors: Yujia Zheng, Tianhao Li, Shangzhi Xu, Yuekai Huang, Ziyou Jiang, Zhaoyang Li, Yuyao Ge, Junran Peng, Mingyang Li
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35082
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35082
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35082]] CoSA: The Conflict of Skills Attack in Real-World LLM Agent Ecosystems(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35082)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Agent Skills allow LLM agents to combine reusable capabilities, but checking each Skill in isolation cannot rule out policy violations when they are composed. Using controlled executions of publicly available Skills and matched single-Skill controls, we identify four recurring mechanisms behind CoS. CoSA leaves existing Skills and runtime permissions unchanged. To evaluate this threat under autonomous Skill selection, we introduce CoSA-Bench, built from real-world Skills and controlled services, with 288 tasks across recruiting, finance, IT, and business, covering data leakage, unauthorized state modification, and unsafe execution. Trace analysis links compliant-to-violating transitions to cross-Skill dependencies involving the additions. Finally, we develop a lightweight harness-level guard that checks information flow and authorization before consequential actions. On OpenClaw recruiting tasks across five models, the guard reduces violations from 65.28% to 6.67%, while policy-compliant task completion on matched benign controls changes from 66.67% to 65.00%.

  • Abstract: Automatic modulation classification (AMC) of radio signals is a core technology for spectrum monitoring and electromagnetic situational awareness. However, their vulnerability to adversarial attacks poses serious security challenges to practical deployment. Compared with PGD-based adversarial training, the proposed method achieves a relative improvement of at least 20.58% in robust accuracy under PGD attacks. When evaluated against attack types unseen during adversarial training, the proposed method provides stronger defense than adversarial training. Title: Spherical Brownian Diffusion for Language Modeling

  • Authors: Andrea Miele, Ilija Bogunovic
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35101
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35101
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35101]] Spherical Brownian Diffusion for Language Modeling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35101)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Continuous diffusion language models involve two coupled design choices: how to corrupt continuous token representations and how to invert that corruption at generation time. Recent work has explored language generation on the unit sphere through geodesic and von Mises--Fisher (vMF) paths, raising the question of which forward process to use on this geometry and how much that choice matters relative to inference. We introduce Spherical Brownian Diffusion, using Brownian motion as the canonical isotropic diffusion on the sphere. Its heat semigroup provides a principled forward process, an additive dimension-free noise clock, and an exact ancestral bridge, while a clean-token posterior learned with cross-entropy supports multiple samplers. To separate corruption from inference, we derive high-dimensional approximation rates between Brownian and moment-matched vMF corruption and use this relationship for controlled substitutions. On Sudoku, Brownian and moment-matched vMF give nearly identical results across four samplers and five budgets under otherwise identical settings. In contrast, with the corruption process fixed, sampler choice produces a 25.0-point spread at a sampler budget of 180 calls. Using the ancestral bridge, we split this gain into a memory effect from re-noising the state, present on Sudoku and GSM8K, and a token-selection effect on GSM8K and largest at small budgets. Across reasoning tasks, categorical refinement improves performance, whereas unconditional text generation benefits from stochastic updates that preserve diversity.

Title: Subspace Crystallization in Grokking: Rank Compression Tracks the Phase Transition

  • Authors: Shlok Mehendale, Paras Chopra
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35112
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35112
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35112]] Subspace Crystallization in Grokking: Rank Compression Tracks the Phase Transition(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35112)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Grokking describes understanding something deeply and intuitively. In neural networks, it is the phenomenon where a network generalizes long after memorizing its training data. This delay has been linked to a fall in the effective rank of the network's weight matrices, but whether this rank compression drives grokking or follows it is still unresolved. We study a mean-field MLP and a one-layer transformer trained on modular arithmetic and observe that: (i) Compression follows the onset, not memorization. Every transformer memorizes its training data within 250 steps, while the grokking onset (the first step at which test accuracy reaches 95%) varies from 6,500 to 21,000 steps. The steepest fall in effective rank comes about 900 steps after the onset, however late the onset occurs, and networks that memorize without grokking compress far less. (ii) Subspace crystallization. We call the span of the fewest top singular directions of a weight matrix that preserve 99% of test accuracy its sufficient subspace. Its size can shrink as the margin between the correct and the best wrong output grows, so we also count the directions needed to keep the outputs within 10%. In the transformer, this count falls during the transition from a quarter or more of the available directions to about a tenth, which we call subspace crystallization. (iii) Imposing low rank does not speed up grokking. Against a factorized full-rank control, capping the rank of the transformer's MLP input weights delays the onset, and capping the rank of its embedding stops 15 of 20 runs from grokking. Compression is therefore a signature of grokking rather than its cause.

  • Abstract: Learning rate decay (LRD) is critical for training high-performance models, yet most designs remain empirically driven. Title: TUON: A Tensor-Aware Optimizer Utilizing Tensor-SVD and Polarization for Large Language Model Pretraining

  • Authors: Anda Tang, Xuanlin Yang, Junjie Wang, Zhouchen Lin
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35130
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35130
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35130]] TUON: A Tensor-Aware Optimizer Utilizing Tensor-SVD and Polarization for Large Language Model Pretraining(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35130)
  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: Large scale language model~(LLM) pretraining is increasingly bottlenecked by optimizer efficiency, driving optimizer design from vector-aware methods such as AdamW to matrix-aware methods. Matrix-aware optimizers such as Muon exploit matrix structure but treat layers independently, neglecting cross-layer correlations. Tensor-aware optimizers are therefore needed, yet existing ones rely on a matricization-based tensor algebra and remain restricted to shallow stacking and specific modules. In this paper, we propose \textbf{TUON}, a novel optimizer built on a transform-based tensor algebra derived from the Tensor-SVD (T-SVD). Unlike methods that polarize a globally unfolded matrix, TUON mixes layers via a unitary transform $\boldsymbol{Q}$ and then polarizes each transformed slice, and inverts the transform to recover the update. This mix-then-separate strategy captures cross-layer correlations while preserving per-layer diversity, avoiding the structural dimension imbalance and cross-layer misalignment of unfolding. Thus it successfully scales to more layers and diverse modules beyond QKV. Theoretically, we prove that TUON performs steepest descent under a $\boldsymbol{Q}$-transformed tensor spectral norm, and identify its update as the tensor polar factor of the T-SVD. We further establish transform non-redundancy, norm and smoothness comparisons with Muon and TEON, and a convergence guarantee. Empirically, TUON improves consistently over Muon and TEON on GPT-124M/350M and Qwen-0.6B/4B, with ablations confirming the transformation mechanism, cross-layer structure beyond two-layer stacking, adaptability beyond QKV, and robustness to the polarization method.

  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often produce plausible but incorrect answers, motivating reinforcement learning methods that reward correct answers, penalize incorrect answers, and allow abstention. However, fixed outcome-based rewards do not distinguish incorrect responses with different confidence levels or account for differences in answering reliability across queries. Consequently, such rewards may inadequately penalize overconfident errors and fail to discourage unnecessary abstention. We propose CalibraRL, a reinforcement learning framework that incorporates response confidence and query-level answering evidence into reward design. For non-abstaining responses, CalibraRL combines correctness-based utility with a Brier-based penalty that encourages agreement between confidence and correctness. For abstentions, it estimates conditional answering reliability using a Beta posterior derived from the correctness of non-abstaining responses within each sampled group. The abstention reward increases with the posterior probability that the model's accuracy on non-abstaining responses is below a predefined threshold. CalibraRL reuses training rollouts without requiring additional annotations indicating whether the model knows the answer to each question. Experiments on three QA datasets demonstrate that CalibraRL improves answer precision and reduces hallucinations compared with strong baselines. Further analysis shows that CalibraRL reduces mean expected calibration error (ECE) by 30.8\% across two backbone models and three datasets, highlighting its potential to improve the reliability of retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) in web search.

Drawing analogies to other fields such as software security, it is often believed that increasing transparency inherently increases the security and trustworthiness of the LLM. However, we argue that such claims need to be critically re-evaluated in the context of LLMs. In this paper, we analyze the impact of transparency on LLM security and trustworthiness by reviewing the relevant literature. Our core findings are that, due to the limited reproducibility of LLM pipelines, in practice, users cannot verify the correspondence between open weights and the associated open data and pipeline. Thus, in order to trust a model, users still need to trust that developers acted honestly, reducing the benefits over models that provide less "transparency". Moreover, we find that even when LLMs are fully transparent and trusted, this openness can favor adversaries. Open weights in particular, but also open data and pipelines, can increase the success rate of attacks. Although the same mechanisms can be used to improve the model and protect it against attacks, the current lack of robust lightweight mechanisms to patch LLM vulnerabilities limits the benefits of openness at this time. We note that our analysis is based on current LLM capabilities and analysis tools, and that future developments may require a re-evaluation of these points.

Title: DTCA: Denoising Token Credit Assignment for Reinforcement Learning in Diffusion Language Models

  • Authors: Hongze Chen, Miao Pan, Lizhou Liang, Qinfeng Li, Xinyin Ma, Yongliang Shen, Weishi Wang, Daniel Dahlmeier, Xuhong Zhang, Wenqi Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35201
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35201
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35201]] DTCA: Denoising Token Credit Assignment for Reinforcement Learning in Diffusion Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35201)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) generate text through iterative denoising, enabling bidirectional context modeling and parallel token refinement. Recent studies have applied reinforcement learning (RL) to DLMs and improved their reasoning performance. However, the iterative denoising process makes credit assignment more challenging, as final outcome rewards provide limited information about how different tokens contribute to the final output. In this paper, we propose Denoising Token Credit Assignment (DTCA), a framework for token-level credit assignment in reinforcement learning for diffusion language models. DTCA estimates token contributions by measuring the improvement in prediction confidence of remaining masked positions after token commitment and groups these signals according to part-of-speech (POS) categories. These contribution weights are used to select informative denoising timesteps and assign token-level learning signals during RL training. DTCA further calibrates these learning signals according to token-level entropy changes induced by policy updates. Experiments on LLaDA-8B-Instruct show that DTCA consistently improves mathematical reasoning performance across different generation budgets, with gains of up to 9.0 points on MATH500 and 5.3 points on GSM8K over the base model.

Title: Efficient Exact Unlearning for Federated Zeroth-Order Optimization

  • Authors: Weijun Yin, Yanqing Yao, Falko Dressler, Di Wang, Youming Tao
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.DC, cs.NI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35211
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35211
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35211]] Efficient Exact Unlearning for Federated Zeroth-Order Optimization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35211)
  • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Federated zeroth-order optimization (FedZOO) reduces the memory and communication costs of collaborative training for edge intelligence by avoiding backpropagation while communicating only random seeds and scalar coefficients. However, existing methods lack efficient mechanisms for exact unlearning to fully remove the influence of training data when users request deletion. A key insight is that total variation stability of the learning algorithm creates overlap between model distributions before and after data deletion, allowing exact unlearning to reuse computation through coupling. Based on this observation, we jointly design two stable zeroth-order learning algorithms and their corresponding exact-unlearning procedures: \textsc{FedZOO-S} obtains stability through client and mini-batch subsampling, enabling \textsc{FedZOUL-S} to perform efficient unlearning by preserving earlier updates and restarting from the first affected step. \textsc{FedZOO-NS} further combines subsampling with Gaussian noise to increase overlap between update distributions; \textsc{FedZOUL-NS} exploits this overlap through rejection--reflection coupling to reuse affected updates when the coupling is accepted. We prove exact unlearning for both algorithms: the model and saved state have the same joint distribution as retraining with the corresponding learner on retained data. We further establish stability and convergence guarantees for the learning algorithms and derive upper bounds on expected unlearning cost. Experiments demonstrate effective unlearning with competitive model utility and up to $56.1\%$ less cumulative unlearning time than re-optimization. Compared with first-order baselines, our methods reduce communication overhead by up to approximately four orders of magnitude.

Title: One-Shot Federated Model Editing for Device Dynamics at the Edge

  • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Standard federated learning (FL) requires persistent device connectivity across multiple communication rounds, making it unsuitable for dynamic environments such as vehicular networks, where devices frequently join and leave. One-shot FL offers a promising alternative by completing training in a single communication round. However, one-shot FL suffers from model solidification and cannot readily adapt to subsequent client dynamics, including new client contributions and the withdrawal of previous contributions. We therefore consider exact model editing, which removes withdrawn contributions through unlearning or incorporates newly contributed ones through incremental learning without retraining from scratch. We identify that efficient editing fundamentally hinges on learning stability. To this end, we propose F-SOSA, the first stable one-shot FL algorithm that enables rapid and exact model editing. F-SOSA leverages sub-sampling of local models with carefully tuned rate for stable aggregation and performs curvature-aware model fusion to preserve utility. Building on this stability, we further develop two single-round editing algorithms: F-SOSA-U for unlearning and F-SOSA-I for incremental learning. They ensure that the edited model, along with all intermediate algorithmic states, is statistically indistinguishable from retraining while incurring only an expected $O(\rho)$ fraction of its cost, where $\rho$ quantifies stability. Experiments demonstrate that our approach matches retraining performance while reducing cumulative time and communication overhead by at least 36% and 22%, respectively, under realistic urban mobility simulations.

In this work, we introduce a controlled experimental framework for studying such effects by systematically perturbing the linguistic style of research papers.   • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Constrained decoding guarantees that Large Language Models (LLMs) produce outputs conforming to predefined formats, but is often assumed to inherently degrade reasoning. We examine this assumption through a taxonomy of three constraint types: \emph{format constraints}, which enforce output structure; \emph{validity constraints}, which mask invalid answer; and \emph{reasoning constraints}, which restrict reasoning trajectories. We theoretically show that appropriate constraints can preserve or improve performance by eliminating invalid paths. Across four model families and eleven open-source models, causal ablations on diverse benchmarks using state-of-the-art constrained decoding strategies reveal that apparent performance degradation largely stems from non-equivalent prompting and output formats rather than from decoder-level masking. Meanwhile, validity constraints improve reliability and extractability by eliminating invalid answers, while reasoning constraints can improve reasoning and, consequently, accuracy when they enforce beneficial trajectories. These findings show that the impact of constrained decoding depends on the type of constraint and the output space it removes. Our code is available.

We evaluated five executable binary vessel-segmentation pipelines (FSG-Net, SA-UNetv2, DCP-1024, GAVE, and FR-UNet) on 244 images from six public datasets using pixel-level agreement, cross-dataset stability, thin-vessel retention, structural metrics, error composition, and available computational evidence. FSG-Net achieved the highest equal-dataset mean Macro-F1 (0.77), IoU (0.63), clDice (0.83), and Skeleton F1 (0.78), whereas SA-UNetv2 achieved the highest mean sensitivity (0.85) and Thin-Vessel Sensitivity (0.63). Differences across datasets were often larger than those among the leading pipelines, and errors shared by all five methods were concentrated near annotated vessel boundaries and faint peripheral vessels. In an ancillary analysis on 45 HRF images, segmentation masks were converted into 11 ring-wise vascular features for leave-one-out classification. Controlled perturbations showed that local mask errors can be amplified in downstream features, while targeted stabilization reduced sensitivity for five features. The results support task-conditioned, benchmark-relative pipeline selection rather than a single universal ranking and highlight the need to validate the selected pipeline on the intended target domain.

Title: SG-Splat: Stable Geometry Learning under Pose Uncertainty for Pose-Free 3D Gaussian Splatting

  • Authors: Xiaohan Lu, Jiaqi Zhang, Weijia Jiang, Siwei Ma
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35361
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35361
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35361]] SG-Splat: Stable Geometry Learning under Pose Uncertainty for Pose-Free 3D Gaussian Splatting(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35361)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has become a promising representation for real-time novel view synthesis and 3D scene reconstruction. For practical deployment, recent studies have explored pose-free feed-forward 3DGS, which reconstructs Gaussian representations directly from unposed input images. However, these methods remain sensitive to residual pose errors, which perturb canonical-space Gaussian transformation, introducing noisy supervision for geometry and attribute learning. To address this problem, we present SG-Splat, a pose-free feed-forward 3DGS framework that improves robustness to pose uncertainty and stabilizes the learning of Gaussian primitives. SG-Splat decouples geometry estimation and Gaussian attribute prediction into two dedicated branches, reducing error coupling between pose-sensitive geometry and Gaussian attributes. We further introduce a coarse-to-fine refinement pipeline, consisting of Flow-Guided Pose Refinement (FGPR) and Residual-Guided Geometry Correction (RGC), to improve geometric alignment before Gaussian attribute reconstruction. Specifically, FGPR refines camera poses with flow-based correlation features and differentiable bundle adjustment. RGC then corrects initial point maps using depth residuals derived from FGPR, producing more reliable Gaussian centers. To stabilize training, we introduce a dual-path strategy that mixes ground-truth and predicted poses for canonical transformation. It provides clean supervision for Gaussian attributes while improving geometry robustness to residual pose errors. Extensive experiments on DL3DV and RealEstate10K show that SG-Splat achieves state-of-the-art performance in both novel view synthesis and pose estimation.

Title: Shared-Factor-Conditioned Diffusion for Low-Rank Tensor Recovery

  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35368
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35368

  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Tensor recovery spans heterogeneous data structures and observation processes, calling for a representation that can adapt across modalities and tasks. We propose DiffTR, an unsupervised framework that unifies tensor completion, denoising, and hyperspectral super-resolution through coordinate-conditioned diffusion. Shared factors indexed by tensor coordinates condition a compact score network, and score matching jointly learns the factors and an implicit tensor representation from the available observations. Scalar and vector-valued outputs accommodate individual entries and entire tubes, enabling a common formulation for tabular tensors, images, and videos. The framework separates representation learning from task-specific observation modeling: reverse diffusion recovers missing values; estimation via Tweedie's formula, soft thresholding, and optional spatial consistency support mixed-noise denoising; and measurement-guided sampling transfers a prior learned from low-resolution hyperspectral observations to high-resolution reconstruction, followed by post-sampling refinement with data consistency and spectral subspace regularization. This separation makes the representation compatible with different corruption models and permits task-specific constraints to be incorporated within the same recovery framework. Experiments across multiple tensor modalities and recovery tasks demonstrate competitive reconstruction quality and the flexibility of a unified conditional generative representation. The code is available at this https URL

  • Abstract: Single-Domain Generalized Object Detection (S-DGOD) aims to generalize from a single source domain to unseen domains under distribution shifts. In this work, we show that unconstrained fine-tuning disrupts the geometric structure of pre-trained representations, which encodes domain-invariant semantics.   • Authors: Adam Hollowell
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35411
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35411

  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: We measure the emotion geometry of six LLMs spanning Google's Gemma 2 family. Using an extraction pipeline established by Sofroniew et al. (2026) on Claude Sonnet 4.5 and replicated by Hollowell (2026) on Gemma 2 27B base, we compare emotion geometry across base and instruction-tuned variants of Gemma 2 2B, 9B, and 27B. In the 2B and 9B models the median emotion word is there from layer 0, while in the 27B models it begins near chance, stays outside the top 10,000 for half the network, and enters at layer 23 in one step. Fourth, tuning leaves every registered analysis-layer measure within its floor. At every size, valence correlation rises and arousal correlation falls by amounts within their floors, the latter against prediction. PC2's variance share also falls, with a pooled decrease significant after correction. Mid-late layer stability exceeds its floor and traces to base 27B's final layer. Code, data, and audit reports are released.

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Photorealistic avatar videos decouple the appearance of a rendered face from the person who operates it, which makes conventional face recognition unsuitable for identifying the operator.   • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Ambiguity in prescriptive text such as laws, contracts, rules, policies, or recommendations can be both desirable (e.g., if it enables a law to adapt to changing context, such as new technologies) and undesirable (e.g., if it leads to arbitrary discretion or conflict over the law's interpretation).   • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35480

  • Abstract: Multi-reward reinforcement learning offers a promising framework for aligning large language models (LLMs) with diverse objectives, but conflicting reward signals can induce ambiguous update directions and hinder stable optimization. Existing approaches address such conflicts through reward reweighting, gradient coordination, or rollout filtering, yet they primarily operate on per-response or group-level optimization signals and underexploit relational information among sampled responses. As a result, conflict-filtered rollouts may still contain useful supervision that is discarded during training. We propose Group Pareto-Dominance Policy Optimization (GPDPO), a multi-reward policy optimization method that recovers pairwise supervision from conflict-filtered rollouts. This enables more effective reuse of existing rollouts without additional sampling or reward evaluation. Experiments on API-Bank across multiple model scales and reward configurations show that GPDPO consistently outperforms competitive multi-reward optimization baselines. Further analysis demonstrates that GPDPO reduces both group-relative and pairwise reward conflicts, supporting the effectiveness of exploiting relational supervision for multi-reward alignment. Title: S$^2$-RL: Sample-Set Dual Reinforcement Learning for Generative Semantic Segmentation Dataset Distillation

  • Authors: Haoyu Wang, Fei Zhou, Qingqing Qiu, Lei Zhang, Wei Wei, Chen Ding
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35491
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35491
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35491]] S$^2$-RL: Sample-Set Dual Reinforcement Learning for Generative Semantic Segmentation Dataset Distillation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35491)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative, segmentation
  • Abstract: While dataset distillation has witnessed significant advances in image classification, its extension to dense prediction tasks such as semantic segmentation is plagued by two core bottlenecks: bi-level optimization-based approaches suffer from prohibitive computational costs stemming from pixel-wise gradient unrolling, whereas proxy-based generative methods merely infuse visual textures into fixed semantic masks derived from real data, thus failing to condense rich semantic knowledge into a compact set of informative, novel scene generations. These design choices enable S$^2$-RL to distill large-scale semantic segmentation datasets into a compact set of generated samples that encapsulate diverse, informative semantic knowledge without the need of pixel-wise gradient unrolling. Extensive experiments on the ADE20K and COCO datasets demonstrate that S$^2$-RL achieves substantial and consistent improvements over state-of-the-art baselines, establishing a new benchmark for SSDD.

Title: PRISMA: Differentially Private Synthetic In-Car Telemetry via Differentiable Simulation

  • Authors: Kilian Zeiseweis, Nele Sophie Brügge, Marven Kummerfeldt, Ali Raza, Esfandiar Mohammadi
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35521
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35521
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35521]] PRISMA: Differentially Private Synthetic In-Car Telemetry via Differentiable Simulation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35521)
  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Differentially private synthesis of in-car energy consumption data is challenging because the output is high-dimensional, and known lower bounds show that the privacy-utility trade-off degrades with the square root of the number of output dimensions. However, we observe that the intrinsic dimensionality of in-car energy consumption is much lower than that of the raw trace: given a small set of contextual personal variables-such as start point, end point, the car model, and the driving behavior-the consumption profile can be largely explained by publicly available map data (including elevation) combined with a physical model of vehicle energy use. We exploit this low intrinsic dimensionality in PRISMA, a Privacy-preserving In-car Synthesis and Modelling Algorithm. PRISMA involves three stages: (i) energy consumption traces are encoded into a low-dimensional latent space; (ii) the distribution of latent vectors is synthesized using the differentially private tabular data synthesis algorithm AIM, and (iii) samples drawn from this distribution are decoded back into full energy consumption traces via a differentiable physics-based simulation, a step that consumes no additional privacy budget since it uses only public information. By using a compact, physically-grounded latent representation of the traces, PRISMA significantly reduces the effective output dimension. Our experimental results show that PRISMA achieves for a competitive $\varepsilon = 0.5$ a better data synthesis performance than prior tabular and marginal-based DP synthesis baselines by preserving the diversity of the original power consumption profiles without collapsing to a small set of modes.

Title: JANUS: Synthesizing Cross-Layer Defenses Against Web Deserialization Attacks

  • Authors: Ruozhao Yang, Minglang Li, Jiongchi Yu, Xinfeng Li, Xiaofei Xie
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35532
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35532
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35532]] JANUS: Synthesizing Cross-Layer Defenses Against Web Deserialization Attacks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35532)
  • Keywords: security, protect, defense, attack
  • Abstract: Unsafe deserialization turns untrusted data into attacker-controlled execution, and a single vulnerability can admit diverse exploit variants that evade defenses tailored to a known exploit. Existing defenses typically operate at a single enforcement point with limited visibility into the full attack. Combining controls across layers offers complementary protection, but coordinating them to preserve legitimate functionality and adapting them to new variants requires substantial expert effort. We propose JANUS, a red-blue adversarial framework for automated cross-layer defense synthesis. Starting from a known exploit, \sys iteratively refines deployment-specific policies in an isolated offline environment. A black-box attacker probes the current policy for exploit variants using only externally observable feedback. The defender summarizes each verified variant into its security-relevant effects, uses counterfactual replay to separate complementary coverage from redundant enforcement, and jointly selects and scopes controls across layers. Each candidate policy is validated against accumulated exploits and benign workflows. We evaluate JANUS on sixteen vulnerability deployments across six Java and PHP applications against gateway, application, system, and cross-layer defenses. JANUS reduces held-out attack success to 6.2%, compared with 18.8-55.0% for the baselines, fully mitigates 13 of 16 deployments, and preserves 98.4% of benign functionality tests at a 6.9% targeted P95 latency increase.

Title: The Agent in the Middle: How AI-Mediated Access Reshapes Web Vulnerability Exploitation

  • Authors: Ruozhao Yang, Jiongchi Yu, Ming Hu, Xiaofei Xie
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35541
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35541

  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: Web services increasingly expose backend interfaces that let users delegate tasks to AI agents, which invoke service operations directly without navigating websites. This shift changes how existing Web vulnerabilities can be exploited. The benchmark preserves vulnerable backends and derives agent-facing interfaces from legitimate workflows. We evaluate three commercial agents across multiple interface configurations, using controlled comparisons to separate interface effects from agent behavior and independent oracles to verify attack outcomes. Our study provides a systematic empirical account of how agent-mediated access reshapes existing Web vulnerabilities, grounding future Web architecture design in measured security outcomes and trade-offs between reducing attack exposure and preserving legitimate functionality in agent-facing services.

Title: StateLoop: Persistent State for Looped Transformers

  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35544]] StateLoop: Persistent State for Looped Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35544)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looped Transformers offer a parameter-efficient approach to language modeling by repeatedly applying a shared backbone. Vanilla looped Transformers use each backbone output directly as the input to the next iteration. These outputs may contain information useful several iterations later, but retaining it across the loop depends entirely on the single hidden state. We introduce StateLoop, which maintains one additional persistent state per token to retain information across iterations. At each iteration, a learned gate controls how much of the previous state is retained and how much information from the backbone output is incorporated. The updated state is projected back and added to the backbone output for subsequent iterations and final prediction. This design reduces Pile perplexity and improves average accuracy across eight zero-shot tasks over Vanilla Looped. Its perplexity advantage over Vanilla Looped grows with trained loop depth, consistent with persistent state becoming increasingly useful as recurrent computation deepens.

Title: Balanced Sampling in Stochastic Multi-objective Bandits

  • Authors: Mengfan Xu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35551
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35551
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35551]] Balanced Sampling in Stochastic Multi-objective Bandits(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35551)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: In stochastic multi-objective bandits, Pareto optimal arms may represent different trade-offs among objectives. Balanced sampling across them is important in practical settings such as online advertising. Yet, there seems to be little consensus on how to measure fairness, to our best knowledge. Achieving fairness is also tough as the set of Pareto optimal arms is unknown, and early sampling decisions may create imbalances that are difficult to erase afterwards. We measure unfairness by the KL divergence from the uniform distribution over the true Pareto set to the empirical measure induced by the sampling trajectory. Nicely, this measure admits an exact decomposition that separates the cost of sampling suboptimal arms from the imbalance among Pareto optimal arms. With a recursive form for unfairness, we develop \emph{adaptive top-two sampling}, which combines sampling counts and empirical evidence of Pareto membership to define a gain for each arm and guide sampling without committing to an estimated Pareto set. When the Pareto set is stable under small perturbations, we establish an $O(\log T/T)$ and $O(T^{-1})$ bound on expected unfairness and variance, respectively. Information-theoretic lower bounds show that the $\log T/T$ rate can be unavoidable for uniformly fair policies on stable instances, matching our upper bound. More surprisingly, stability is indeed crucial, as the expected unfairness can be $\omega(\log T/T)$ without it. Finally, we create a variant that encourages exploration of undersampled arms to reduce the risk of persistent underallocation. At sufficiently large horizons, it preserves the rate on the expected unfairness while admitting a sharper bound of $O(\log^2 T/T^2)$ on variance. Numerical studies support these findings and suggest practical insights.

Title: LoopFold: Parallelizing Recurrent Depth in Looped Transformers

  • Authors: Feichen Song, Boyi Zeng, Yiqin Hao, Shixiang Song, He Li, Yizhou Yang, Zitong Wang, Yifan Liu, Ziwei He, Zhouhan Lin
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35556
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35556
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35556]] LoopFold: Parallelizing Recurrent Depth in Looped Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35556)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looped Transformers increase computation per token by repeatedly applying a shared backbone, offering a way to improve language modeling and downstream task performance at a fixed backbone parameter count. However, each loop depends on the preceding loop, so increasing recurrent depth also increases decoding latency. Existing approaches include Parallel Loop Transformers, which reduce latency by overlapping loop computation across token positions, but yield rapidly diminishing marginal accuracy gains at larger depths. To address this challenge, we introduce LoopFold, which folds computation at multiple recurrent depths into parallel batches for the same token prefix. A lightweight predictor estimates inputs to later loops so they can start before earlier loops finish. A corrector then refines the outputs of these later loops using newly available results from earlier loops. All components are trained jointly using only the final next-token prediction loss. In experiments with 1.4B models, deeper LoopFold configurations achieve progressively higher average zero-shot accuracy across eight tasks, rising from 53.41% to 56.38%. Vanilla looped models and PLT show limited gains over the same depth range. Compared with a nine-loop vanilla model, LoopFold uses five sequential stages and additional parallel computation to reduce decoding latency by 46%.

Title: Copying As Defense: Protecting Images from Editing By Eliciting Model Copying

  • Authors: Salma Abdel Magid, Sanghyuk Chun, Eric Slyman, Kushal Kafle, Olga Russakovsky
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35566
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35566
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35566]] Copying As Defense: Protecting Images from Editing By Eliciting Model Copying(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35566)
  • Keywords: protect, defense, diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Image editors built on diffusion transformers (DiTs), such as Flux Kontext, make it possible to turn a single photograph into a convincing manipulated image, including harmful and non-consensual edits. Proactive defenses aim to prevent such manipulations by adding an imperceptible perturbation before an image is shared. Existing defenses for DiT-based in-context editors make the output attend less to the protected reference image. But this leaves the model free to follow the edit instruction using the text and its own generated tokens, often producing a new, weakly grounded image rather than preventing the edit. We find that the opposite strategy is more effective: instead of leading the model to ignore the reference, we encourage it to copy it. Through controlled attention interventions, we show that concentrating target-token attention on the reference image in only a few early blocks is sufficient to collapse the editing behavior, causing the model to reproduce its input, often as a lossy copy, rather than execute the requested transformation. Based on this insight, we introduce Copying-as-Defense (CaD), which maximizes per-token target-to-reference attention mass in early transformer blocks. Since attention rows are normalized, increasing reference attention suppresses the attention allocated to the text and generated canvas. Restricting the objective to early blocks also lets us truncate the backpropagation path, keeping optimization cheap. Across editors and benchmarks, CaD reduces average edit success from 81.4% on unprotected images to 24.0%, a 70.6% relative reduction, and by a further 44.3% relative to DeContext, the strongest baseline (43.0%). CaD is also roughly 1.8-2.0x faster per optimization step than comparable methods.

  • Abstract: Few-step distillation is essential for deploying modern video diffusion models. Among existing approaches, Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) has emerged as a highly promising paradigm by achieving strong output-space fidelity without adversarial training, while remaining flexible across various student architectures. We observe, however, that DMD fundamentally relies on an output-space objective, lacking explicit regularization on the model's internal representations. As the distillation step count aggressively shrinks, this output-only supervision becomes insufficient, causing the distilled student to degrade in both fine-grained spatial structures and coherent temporal dynamics. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SF$^2$ effectively mitigates temporal flickering and consistently improves both spatial fidelity and motion quality. Furthermore, the proposed mechanism is architecture-agnostic - seamlessly supporting both bidirectional and causal models - and requires no external feature extractors, introducing minimal computational overhead.

Title: Active Learning of Conditional Generative Models via the Transport Neural Tangent Kernel

  • Authors: Jayoung Ryu, Kyunghyun Cho, Romain Lopez
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35652
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35652
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35652]] Active Learning of Conditional Generative Models via the Transport Neural Tangent Kernel(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35652)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Many high-throughput scientific experiments produce distributional outputs, requiring conditional generative models to interpret the data, and to predict outcomes at new inputs. Acquiring experimental data at new conditions (e.g., for a new antigen or a new cell type) is often expensive, motivating the need for automated experimental design methods. However, existing active learning frameworks are limited as they assume scalar or vector-valued outputs. We first show that the Wasserstein test risk of a conditional generative task decomposes into a mean term and a shape term, demonstrating why mean-based active learning is blind to distributional shape. To capture both, we propose the transport Neural Tangent Kernel (tNTK), a tractable kernel that measures how sensitive a generative model's predicted distribution is with respect to its parameters. The tNTK recovers the standard NTK in the deterministic limit and upper-bounds the posterior Fréchet variance of the prediction under small parameter perturbations. It admits a closed-form expression for affine Gaussian transport and tractable Monte Carlo estimators otherwise. Treating the tNTK as the covariance function of a Gaussian process, we greedily select inputs that minimize the total GP posterior variance across the pool, yielding a distribution-aware active learning strategy. We evaluate this strategy across four classes of conditional generative models, including conditional flow matching and conditional Monge gap, on synthetic distributions, intent-labeled sentences, and single-cell perturbation responses. In these experiments, the tNTK outperforms the baseline NTK computed from the conditional mean, demonstrating the value of distribution-aware active learning for generative-modeling tasks.

  • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: One of the main challenges of federated learning is heterogeneous data split among the clients, leading to biased local updates called client drift. The SCAFFOLD algorithm (Karimireddy et al., 2020) corrects client drift with control variates, but its relation to the similarity of client data is not yet fully understood: the original similarity-based guarantees cover only quadratics with a stronger second-order similarity assumption, and a follow-up weakly convex analysis (Luo et al., 2025) obtains a $\sqrt{L\delta}$ dependence for $L$-smooth losses with averaged Hessian similarity $\delta$. We analyze SCAFFOLD with minibatch-based control variates and establish linear dependence on Hessian similarity beyond quadratics, for both convex and nonconvex objectives. In particular, for convex objectives with exact gradients, $R$ SCAFFOLD rounds with $K$ local steps lead to an objective gap of order $(L/K+\delta)D_0^2/R$, where $D_0$ is the initial distance to a minimizer. When $K=\Theta(L/\delta)$, it improves the minibatch SGD rate by a factor $\Theta(L/\delta)$. For nonconvex objectives, we prove an analogous stationarity guarantee under uniform Hessian similarity for a client-selection variant. In both settings, we extend these guarantees to stochastic gradients and prove matching lower bounds for the analyzed methods with constant local stepsizes. We also show that, under partial participation, even identical clients need not obtain the full communication benefit of additional local steps.
