Title: Triage: Skip, Skim, or Refine Attention Tiles in Video Diffusion Transformers

  • Authors: Yeonjae Kim, Omin Kwon, Seong Hoon Seo, Jae W. Lee
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35706
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35706
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35706]] Triage: Skip, Skim, or Refine Attention Tiles in Video Diffusion Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35706)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Attention over tens of thousands of visual tokens dominates the inference of video diffusion transformers (DiTs). Existing methods reduce this cost through sparsity, low precision, or both, but use a single precision for all kept tiles. We observe that the output error introduced by approximation varies widely across tiles. Many tiles can be skipped with little error, others must be computed but gain little beyond low precision, and a few retain substantial error even at low precision. We present Triage, a training-free attention method that assigns each tile one of three approximation tiers: skipping it by replacing it with its block means, skimming it in NVFP4, or refining it with NVFP4 residual correction. A lightweight router predicts each tile's error under each tier from block statistics and moves a tile to a more accurate tier only when the predicted error reduction justifies the extra compute, with a single price setting this trade-off. A fused kernel executes all three tiers in a single pass on the FP4 tensor cores of RTX Blackwell GPUs. Across Wan2.2, HunyuanVideo, and LTX-2.5, Triage accelerates end-to-end generation by up to 2.32x over dense FlashAttention, and in each of five settings it runs faster than every baseline while reaching 0.3-3.0 dB higher PSNR than the highest-PSNR baseline.

On one hand, one must examine in-distribution statistical generalisation, which concerns the ability of a model to broaden a function from training samples to novel samples of the same distribution.   • Abstract: Data distributions often possess one or more symmetries: for example, all orientations of any specific dog are included in the distribution of all possible dog pictures.   • Abstract: Preference optimization can learn spurious correlations, and augmenting the preference data with ties (equally preferred responses) can reduce spurious reliance. However, ties differ in their corrective effect, making their selection for augmentation important. For log-linear policies, we derive each tie's influence on spurious reliance and show that taking the largest predicted reductions is first-order optimal among subsets of equal size. Computing these influences requires knowledge of the spurious features and the curvature of the training objective, which is often unavailable or expensive to estimate. To test the theory and its qualitative transfer to neural models, we estimate influence with a counterfactual proxy for spurious reliance and use a generalized Gauss--Newton approximation to curvature for language models. Across log-linear policies, neural models, and large language models, our selection rules consistently achieve comparable reductions in spurious reliance with fewer ties than uniform selection.

  • Abstract: Deep research, or any use of large language models (LLMs) to read a large document collection and produce insights, requires synthesizing information from different sources. We propose dSpark, a new schedule distillation-based framework for learning when to commit tokens for faster diffusion language generation. Title: Understanding Jev-Style System One Models: Does Structure Reduce Selection Bias?

  • Authors: Mingyang Song, Mao Zheng, Zhiyuan Ma, Xinpeng Zhou
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35826
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35826
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35826]] Understanding Jev-Style System One Models: Does Structure Reduce Selection Bias?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35826)
  • Keywords: protect, generative, large language model
  • Abstract: Selection bias in large language models (LLMs) can persist despite instructions to ignore presentation order or prompts that elicit reasoning. Recent efforts to reproduce Jev-style System One behavior have produced a growing family of models that predict probabilities directly over supplied candidates. Yet systematic comparisons of selection bias across Jev versions, Jev reproductions, and generative LLMs remain limited in multi-option tasks. This leaves a central question: does Jev-style decision structure reduce selection bias? We address this question with a simple test that shuffles options while keeping question and candidate content fixed. Across two Jev versions, twelve Jev reproductions, and six generative LLMs, we trace individual answers through eight presentations and compare them with fixed-order repetition. On our primary collection of 1,700 questions from two benchmarks, Jev's accuracy varies by less than two percentage points, yet its cumulative failure set expands by 27--29\% relative to the original order, with first failures continuing into the final presentation. This loss of initial successes is substantially larger than under fixed-order repetition, even for highly repeatable Jev reproductions. Nor does Jev show a general advantage over the LLM baselines in preserving correct choices. Aggregation offers limited protection: voting yields modest gains for Jev, while even unanimous answers are frequently wrong. These results expose a gap between repeatable decisions and decisions that remain correct across equivalent presentations, showing why stable accuracy alone cannot establish resistance to selection bias.

Title: Reva: Real-Time Vulnerability Detection through Contextual Probing of Large Language Models

  • Authors: Amine Lbath, Massih-Reza Amini
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35829
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35829
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35829]] Reva: Real-Time Vulnerability Detection through Contextual Probing of Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35829)
  • Keywords: security, defense, large language model
  • Abstract: A vulnerability costs more to fix the later it is discovered. As coding agents generate code faster than developers can review it, software needs a first line of defense screening each new function in real time. LLM agents have recently become capable of finding real-world vulnerabilities by working as a security expert would: they run analysis tools, explore the surrounding code, test hypotheses and weigh the evidence before forming their own judgement. This reasoning, however, spans many model calls, so that each verdict takes minutes of compute, far too long for real-time screening. Faster detectors, which fine-tune deep learning models or prompt an LLM for a direct verdict, are less accurate: they judge a function without the context an agent gathers, and an LLM's generated verdict is unreliable even when given some of it. We present REVA (REal-time Vulnerability Analysis), an AI vulnerability detector that brings the security context and code understanding components behind agentic detectors into a single forward pass of an LLM. For each new function, REVA gives the LLM a pre-computed threat model of its module, lightweight static analysis findings and call-graph information. REVA leverages a trained probe to read the LLM's internal representations of the new function in this context. On PrimeVul's held-out test set, REVA on GLM-5.3-Flash reaches 60.0% accuracy in about one second per function. This matches VulTriage (59.2%), the state-of-the-art agentic detector, run on the same LLM, while being over 250 times faster and less than a tenth as costly. On the same prompt, the probe is 14% to 17% more accurate than the LLM's own direct verdict. Across three open LLMs, REVA is at least as accurate as VulTriage, and about 10% more accurate on two of them. An LLM's internal representations thus encode much of what agentic detectors obtain through reasoning.

Title: I-HITL: Inalienable Human-in-the-Loop

  • Authors: Samuel Breckenridge, Dani Vilardell, Wenhao Wang, Fan Zhang, Ari Juels
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35835
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35835
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35835]] I-HITL: Inalienable Human-in-the-Loop(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35835)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Human oversight is a pillar of AI safety. It is often exercised by a human in the loop (HITL) who approves critical actions taken by an AI agent. AI agents' increasing ability to bypass guardrails, though, raises the question of how to enforce HITL robustly. We introduce the concept of inalienable human-in-the-loop (I-HITL). An I-HITL protocol ensures that an agent can initiate an action only with explicit human approval, even when the human interacts with a system (e.g., a local agentic harness) compromised by the agent. A strong I-HITL protocol additionally ensures such approval even when an adversarial human attempts to delegate approvals to an agent. To motivate I-HITL, we report on experiments with a popular agentic wallet platform in which raising an agent's spending cap requires HITL approval via emailed one-time passcodes (OTPs). Given access to the user's email and a task blocked by the cap, we find that ten different frontier models will often autonomously raise the cap, harvesting OTPs to bypass a HITL guardrail and surfacing other vulnerabilities. We formally define I-HITL using the concept of communication topologies and prove necessary and sufficient conditions for its realization. We show that an I-HITL protocol requires a trusted entity - a trusted computing device (or equivalent) - and characterize which such devices can and cannot realize an I-HITL protocol. We present a prototype I-HITL system called Countersign that can plug into and strengthen existing HITL systems using off-the-shelf consumer mobile devices.

Title: Preserve to Expand: Source-Aware Adaptation and Model Merging for Multilingual LLM-Based ASR

  • Authors: Yuchen Zhang, Haralambos Mouratidis, Ravi Shekhar
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35846
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35846
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35846]] Preserve to Expand: Source-Aware Adaptation and Model Merging for Multilingual LLM-Based ASR(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35846)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Expanding multilingual ASR systems to new languages is challenging when adaptation must preserve previously supported languages without replaying source-language training data. We study this problem in connector-based Speech LLMs, where a trainable connector maps speech representations into a frozen large language model. We evaluate target-update construction and merging strategies for adapting the connector while preserving source-language performance. In single-target expansion, target-only fine-tuning and standard TIES merging learn new languages but cause severe source-language forgetting, while SSU-Wanda+TIES gives the best source-preserving trade-off. In multi-target expansion, update merging provides stable source retention, whereas recursive expansion can improve target adaptation under favorable orders but is highly order-sensitive. These results show that multilingual ASR expansion requires balancing target acquisition, source retention, and interactions among target-language updates.

Title: OSWAM: An Effective World Action Model with One-Step Action Generation

  • Authors: Guowen Zhang, Liyi Chen, Xu Hu, Pengfei Wang, Jingfan Chen, Chenhang He, Lei Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35850
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35850
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35850]] OSWAM: An Effective World Action Model with One-Step Action Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35850)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Direct-policy World Action Models (WAM) have demonstrated the benefits of decoupling action generation from explicit future visual prediction for efficient inference in autonomous driving and embodied control. However, they still require tens of denoising steps to achieve competitive action quality, incurring substantial latency and hindering real-time deployment. Simply reducing the number of denoising steps will collapse the nonlinear multi-step denoising trajectory into coarse updates, substantially degrading action quality. To reduce the denoising steps while enabling real-time action generation, we present an effective world action model with one-step action generation, namely OSWAM, for autonomous driving and embodied control. We argue that it is the absence of structured action priors in random noise initialization that forces direct-policy WAMs to recover coherent action sequences through iterative denoising. Hence, we construct imagination tokens to initialize the diffusion process, providing physical priors and action-conditioned representations. We further introduce privileged information distillation with future conditioning to enhance future-aware action generation. As a result, OSWAM can efficiently and effectively generate high quality actions in just one diffusion step. Our experiments demonstrate that OSWAM achieves comparable or even better results in both autonomous driving and embodied control than direct-policy WAM and Joint-WAM that require tens of steps. It obtains approximately $3\times$ speedup over direct-policy WAM baselines and supports real-time compiled inference with latency as low as 40ms on an NVIDIA RTX 5090 GPU. The project page is available at this https URL

Title: VibeVideoBench:Evaluating Multimodal Agents on Code-Driven Video Generation

  • Authors: Muxi Diao, Wenbin Xu, Lele Yang, Xuyang Ma, Yuzhe Chen, Jiankang Chen, Kaiyu Jiang, Changyi Liu, Haoyu Zhang, Hongyi Fu, Jianxiong Wang, Tianke Zhang, Haonan Fan, Yingxin Li, Bingyi Li, Xu Wang, Xuanyu Zheng, Bin Wen, Tingting Gao, Han Li, Wentao Zhang, Weiran Xu, Kongming Liang, Zhanyu Ma
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35864
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35864
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.35864]] VibeVideoBench:Evaluating Multimodal Agents on Code-Driven Video Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35864)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion video models produce striking clips, but they cannot reliably render an exact formula, a correct chart, a ruleconsistent game or narration synchronized to on-screen events. A different route is emerging: multimodal agents that write code to render every frame and synthesize every sound. We introduce VibeVideoBench, a benchmark for evaluating agents on code-driven video generation. Its 239 self-contained tasks in English and Chinese span 12 categories, from data stories and music-driven pieces to classroom lessons and paper explainers; each asks an agent, running in its native harness inside a sandbox, to deliver a finished MP4 with sound within one hour. Evaluation is hierarchical and evidence-based: program checks of the delivery spec, about 13k task-specific rubric checks traced to the requirements they enforce, and five quality dimensions anchored per task, judged by three VLM judge panels and validated with blind human preferences. Across nine frontier agent systems and 2,151 scored rollouts, the benchmark is far from solved: GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 lead clearly, yet even the best system fails a core requirement on about a quarter of tasks. Whether a video arrives at all matters as much as its quality, and three quarters of the lost videos are final renders started too late, run too slowly or never started. The judges agree with each other and with human preferences on system rankings, but only moderately on individual videos.
